WOMEN'S CHAT.

Vindictive Wills.—The vicissitudes of matrimony are frequently reflected invindictive wills. Sometimes it is merely a piece of biting sarcasm, that accompanies a substantial bequest. A short of parting shot but not a legacy of iil.will. That, however, can hardly be said of the will, recently proved, in which the testator referred to his wife as a" perambulating human vinegar cruet." There is nothing of the emphemism about that, as there was in the action of the man who bequeathed a large fortune to a lady, as he stated in his will, out of gratitude for her refusal to marry him twenty years before. That is gilding the pill in a way which makes it easy to swallow. Sales of Wives.—The old idea that woman is man's property was recently recalled by the authentic case in Essex in which the wife had been sold by the husband to another man for £5. There are records of old wills in which the husband has bequeathed his wife to another man, and well into the nineteenth century it was commonly supposed among the ignorant that a sale of a wife was legal, provided that the price was more than one and the woman was delivered to her purchaser with a%alter round her neck. According to contemporary accounts, the woman was seldom an unwilling party to the bargain. The French used to refer to this custom in speaking disparagingly of us as a people, but the Napoleonic wars had most to do with gaining us the credit of being a nation of wife-sellers. Soldiers returning home after a long absence found that their wives had been pei-suaded they were dead and bad married again. More often than not, the affair was settled by a money payment, and so frequent were these occurrences that whenever possible, the authorities shut their eye; to the proceedings. Wintering in Switzerland.—Of recent years Switzer- land has become very popular as a winter resort for leisured people. The season extends from the middle of December to the early part of February, the climate being of the dry cold bracing de-cription, the air like champagne, and the pri icipal amusements being skating, ski-ing, and tobaganning. It is not necessarily I expensive, and one can live at the chief hotels in a place like Grindlewald for n or 15 francs a day, or even less. As the Arab says "he that drinks of the waters of the Nile will return again," and those who have spent one winter holiday in Switzerland generally speak enthusiastically about it, and want another. Nothing special is required in the way of clothing. In the ordinary way, the usual winter costumes of tweed 1 or serge, such as we wear at home are all that are : wanted. A knitted coat, and an extra short skirt or ; two, should be taken for skating, and in fact the ordinary winter golfing suit is an ideal costume for a 1 sporting holiday of this kind. A big, easy fitting warm ' tweed coat, is of course indispensable, but although it , is cold weather, it is the sort that never feels cold, and nothing heavier or warmer than we are accustomed to at home is necessary. A healthy Exercise.—Fencing finds many advocates ' nowadays, but unfortunately, it is one of the most expensive forms of exercise. Still much may be

===== 0002601_19081114_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
ASK YOUR FOR McDOUGALL'S Patelit -SELF—RAISING FLOUR The Examiner Saturday, November 14, 1908 WOMAN'S REALM. DO YOU FEAR THE WINTER? ______ TEMPERANCE SUNDAY.

amomplished without the costly lessons from an expert. The windows of the room in which a woman practises, should be thrown open to admit plenty of fresh air, then with a long flexible foil, she should endeavour to touch the same spot in a target. In this—the attack, the weight is thrown upon the right foot, while the balancing is accomplished with the left arm, the head is erect, the shoulders straight. It is claimed that this position decreases the size of the waist and hips—an item worth noting by lovers of the Direotoire mode— that it develops the arm and wrist, and all the muscles of the back. Aids to Beauty.—One would think that the periodical exposure of so-called "beauty doctors," without any special qualifications is ever increasing, and the business as far as they are concerned is usually a flourishing one. There are legitimate aids to beauty, by which one may seek to give a softness to the skin, clearness to the complexion, and a gleam to the hair, but when it comes to injecting colouring matter under the skin, the plumping out of cheeks by injecting paraffin wax, and the use of chemicals and instruments, there are grave risks that the ultimate result will be a face hope- lessly marred and disfigured. The sufferer as a rule, does not care to take any steps which would show her up as having been victimised, but now and again a case comes into Court, as happened the other day when substantial damages were recovered, against a " face specialist." Muffs.—The epidemic of freak fashions has extended to muffs, which are preposterously huge, and of the fiat, limp order, suggestive of the "tea cosy." Many are adorned with masks and tails, the furs most in favour being fox, skunk, and ermine. No doubt motoring has set the fashion for huge muffs, and for this purpose they might almost be called muff-rugs. Some have an extra big flaps, which can be unfastened and spread over the knees, whilst they are so wide that the arms as well as the hands can be tucked into them, They are all very well for winter motoring, but or shopping the huge muff is a bit of a nuisance, and for ordinary purposes those of only moderate dimensions are more convenient. Unsuitable Modes.—The very wide flat hat is being enthusiastically taken up by the masses" which means a prompt disappearance of this form of headgear among well-dressed women. Motor omnibuses, tram-cars, and railway carriages, are full of these wide flat bats, and it is often amusing to watch the wearers' endeavours to steer clear of their fellow passengers, and their disgust when somebody else's brims work havoc with their own. Thus, once again, the fact is brought fully before us, that modes suitable for the wealthy are often extremely unsuitable for women who have to crowd together and travel in public vehieles' but all this seems to be little taken to heart by working !girls and women. About Yokes.—The passing of the white ard cream yokes is to be deplored, as few fashions have proved more generally becoming. Of course when the bodice happens to be white or cream, the lace, embroidery, net, etc., that forms the yoke, matches, but otherwise the yoke takes the same hue as the fabric of which the blouse is made, the only relief being that afforded by the white neck frill. Fragile-looking Jewellery.—The engagement ring most sought after this season is the circlet of small diamonds, the half-hoop of large stones being less seen than formerly, and massive ornaments are deemed bad taste. Jewellery, generally speaking, is of the fragile order, a state of affairs calculated to delight the artistic community. Toyland.—The toy bazaars promise a more tempting display than ever this coming festive season. Every year we have toys of greater variety and ingenuity, made to suit all pur,--es,and in this respec t the little folks of the pre=ent day are much more fortunate than were those of former generations. It is true that the doll, the doll's house, the Jack-in-the-box, the drum, and the wooden horse, all retain their old favour, and appear year after year with minor changes, but in addition we have greater delights and wonders offered us in an infinite variety of ingenious mechanical toys. A feature this season will be the imitation flying machine, of which we shall see all sorts and hinds. The suffragists are also turned to account, and are presented arguing with a Cabinet Minister in the witness-box. Flip-flaps and spiral railways are amongst the mechanical toys, while dancing pigs andThears, gobbling or walking birds, and animals' all with something to do, or with a "speaking part," figure amongst the allurements and temptations presented at the toy bazaars on every side. MADGE.

Those of our readers who are suffering from any of the ills arising from Anemia (Bloodlessness), should communicate with Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., 46, Holborn Viaduct, London. All letters receive immediate attention.

Watchman Dies at His Post.

At the Police Station on Monday Er. F. A. Jones deputy coroner, held an inquest concerning the death of George Worthington (60), a night watchman, employed by Mr. William Cameron, general galvanizer, Mottram's-yard, Scotland-road.—Joseph Miller, 10, Mitchell-street, Stockton Heath, said be was also employed at the same works, and at ten minutes to six o'clock on Saturday morning he found the deceased lying on his stomach in the yard. Both his hands were clenched, and when he examined the body he found that life was extinct.— Dr. Smith said there was no maths of violence on the -body. Death was due to failure of the heart's action due to congestion of the lungs. The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the medical testimony.

TIIE VAGARIES OF TASEION.

The chief sartorial effort in life at the moment seems to be the achievement of an extremely flat back below the waist, wherever that debatable line is placed. Sans hire, sans bustle, cans everything that makes for grace- ful undulettien, we are verily a cemmunity of swayire, eaplinge. To this edict, both toilers and nadietes havereuecumbed, writes Mrs. Jack May ie the Bystander, the former proclaim- ing themselves immensely cenc.erred over the manipulation of a basque •to the approved long coat, usually An applied affair, ostensibly held in Position by clusters of buttons. Viewed squarely from the beck, the eye is not dleesed by that flat arriere aspect, whereas taken en profile there is frequently an utter lack of grace, .the hips, even in their sup- pressed condition forming a prominence by comparison with the sunk-en beck. Pondering these vagaries, cue is seriously led to won- dering whether La Mode is act, peradventure, foofing'with us, testing our powers of gulli- bility, and having got us to straining point, will ineke a volte face end enjoy a laugh at our eipense. Meanwhile, the oftentime valu- able male testimony as to what 'conetitutes elegance is waved away with scorn. Woman is entirely emancipated where dress is con- cerned, and with few exceptione entirely in- different as to Whether she courts approval or the reverse at the hands of her men appen- dages. And men, taken en bloc, are securing much amusement at our expense. • THE LATEST SKIRT.

Our sketch illustrates the very latest thing in Skirts. It is a perfectly new model, haing from Paris, and promises to be one cf the most popular and fashionalle models for walking wear during th 2 coming winter sea- son. This skirt is really a circular affair, with a seam down the middle of the back and a panel inserted down the front. This panel, you will observe,- has the edges of the skirt turned over it on each side, and decorated

with a row of rather large buttons, each button being furnished with a large buttonhole or a loop of cord or satin tubing. But the feature of the skirt is the high top, which is cut to come well above the waist in regular corselet style. The front It 3 prolonged into a , little point that riees above the level of the, rest of the skirt, and is intended to hook neatly on to the blouse or corkage with which the skirt is worn. THE EVENING SCARF: ' The scarf as worn in the daytime has had one success to 9C9 failures, declares a Globs writer. Chosen haphazard, chiefly from ad-; miration of its colour without consideration of its effect upon the complexion of the wearer; usually badly worn, it was difficult for it to avoid being a failure. Now, however, the floating scarf for outdoor wear is hardly in season. Those for evening are often beau- tiful. For instance a white Chantilly bor- dered with a narrow ruching of turquoise chiffon; a cream-coloured gauze edged with a line of skunk or sable; a rippled pale green silk crepe finished with a hemstitch; a pale sea-pink silk muslin edged with soft brown silk; a grey-blue painted in green and blue and pale mauve flowers; a pure white chiffon edged with fine black lace. Lovely is a scarf, some three yards long, in silk Maltese dyed to a delicious tea-tint and, lined with palest rose China silk. Those who admire jewelled lace would like a scarf in finest imi- tation Venice point all a-glitter with rubies, emeralds, and diamonds, none of which have erer seen a mine. TO-DAY'S RECIPE.'

GIBLET PlE.—Take two sets of giblets nicely clenned,.put all but the liver into a saucepan, with two quarts of water, twenty corns of whole pepper, three blades of mace, a bundle of sweet herbs, and a large onion. Cover them close, let them stew very softly till they are quite tender. Have a Food crust ready, line the inside of the dish with it; lay a fine rump-steak at the bottom, seasoned with pepper and salt; then lay in the gimlets 'with the liver. PEPS FOR COUGHS AND COLDS.

If you are among the many who have good cause, from sad experience, to fear the shortening days, the approach of winter, the chilly nights, broken weather, keen winds, fog, rain, frost and storm, now is tilt, time to arm yourself with a genuine remedy for throat and chest troubles.

_ There are two great Causes to which all chest and throat troubles are traceable. First, infections epidemics, spread by germs, to which all are more or less subject; and second, weakness of the throat and chest, due to some neglected or wrongly treated trouble in former seasons.

The only sensible way to fight the first of these evils - the risk of catching infectious colds, influenza, or contagious throat trouble -is to keep Peps always at hand in the home, as the only reliable and certain safeguard which is free from nerve-destroying drugs and habit-forming narcotics. All germ-carried diseases are breathed into the body,inhaled through the nostrils and throat into the lungs and so absorbed into the blood. The germ-killing fumes of Peps are also breathed into the throat and lungs in the same way. The disme is thus attacked and conquered by Peps along its own line of assault. Other medicines conveyed to the blood through the stomach -like liquid cough-mixtures -only begin their feeble attack on the disease when the mischief is already done, when the germs have invaded the blood and infected the whole body, and then it is too late. Whenever a change in the weather, or any indiscreet or unavoidable exposure to chills, draughts, night air, or sudden change of temperature, threaten a return of old throat or chest troubles, a few Peps tablets slowly dissolved in the month will fill the lungs with their healing and balsamic essences. As these wonder- ful medicinal fumes are breathed into every corner of our lungs and chest, the delicate tissues are gently stimulatedinto glowing natural action, repelling chill, softening accumulated phlegm, loosening the tight- ness" of the breathing passages, and toning up the weak spots. Of all the thousand and one" remedies" so vainly used for coughs, colds, bronchitis, asthma, influenza, croup, wheeziness, tightness, tieklina cough, night cough, spasmodic cough, barking, hawking, hoarseness, ' sore throat huskiness, loss of voice, stiff ness in the chest, no medicine has achieved such universal success as Peps. . . .

_ Delicate children, racked with terrible coughs (and -only anxious mothers know how pitifully the children suffer from such evils), get the great benefit from the soothing influences of Peps. Old folks, whose days and nights are one long torture from chronic deep- seated bronchial evils, find peace comfort, and cure in Peps. PAIR HALL.

Grand Scottish Concert.

At the instance of the Warrington Caledonian Association a concert, in which the principal vocalists were pretty well all Scotch and the music mostly so, was given on Monday evening in Parr Hall to a large audience. The items in the first part of the programme were of a somewhat melancholy character and gave to the concert an unnecessarily serious tone which threatened to develop into dulness, but the cloud was happily dispelled as the evening wore on. Miss Jessie Mc Lachlan, announced as "The Queen of Scottish Song," fairly demonstrated her right to the title. The possessor of a bright soprano voice, well under control, she sang with a dramatic fervour and an intensity of feeling "The Auld Scotch Sangs," (Bethune)" We re a hundred pipers sea" and two other kindred things in the original Gaelic, in a manner which, literally thialed the audience. It seemed quite in the na'ule of things that, not being included in the programme, we should have " Coming thro' therm " as an encore for the soprano, just as we bad Annie Laurie " after the recall of the baritone. But MissllcLachlan's rendeting of " Gin a 'body meet a body " was so clever a mixture of the piquant, the demure, and the ; blithesome, that the thing was quite refreshing in its newness, and it is seldom a well worn favourite was so much enjoyed. The contralto vocalist Madame Marion Christie sang. " Braw, braw lads " and Roekell's well-known Angus Macdonald " with a ' full firm and resonant tone and that conscientious ' grasp of the spirit of these songs which marks the true artiste, and which called forth the warmest ; plaudits. Mr. George Neil, the tenor, was not altogether successful either in his scotch billads or in his interpretation of such a quite ordinary English sentimental song as Stephen Adams's "Maid of Malabar." In the upper reaches his voice is some- what hard and unsympathetic and when he is not disconcei tingly violent in his tone gradations and marks of expression he so far falls short of realislng any real depth of feeling as to border on flippancy. Yet, curiously enough, Mr. Neil was most felicitously paired with Miss McLachlan in the duet "The crooket bawbee" which proved a veritable comedy in minia-; ture,so full was it'of incident and by-play. The thing was hugely enjoyed and caused quite an outburst of enthusiasm. The baritone Mr. Robert Burnett proved himself a most capable singer. In the contrasted and coupled songs "The Sands o' Dee" (Clay) and "Marching along" (M. V. White) one had ample proof of his versatility and his deep insight into the aitistic possibilities of such compositions. Rarely have we heard the def.p pathos of the first mentioned song so tellingly brought out. Mr. Theo. Crozier, a young violinist who is rapidly winning renown, played Sarasate's " Zigeunerweisen ' and the Romance from Wieniawski's second concerto in a manner which holds cut the most encouraging promises for his future. His style has both breadth, animation and soulfulness,and his technique is in many respects admirable. But while his habits ate yet in a state of youthful plasticity it would he well for Mr. Crozier to guard against the particular one of throwing himself about in a distracting manner during the performance of his solos. It should be mentioned that Mr. Crozier was ably backed up at the piano by Mr. W. H. Payton Mus. Bac. Some humorous sketches of more that ordinary interest by Mr. Craighall Sherry gave variety to the evening's entertainment.

TWENTY YEARS AFTER.

Public opinion is proverbially short-lived. The topic which is on everyone's lips to-day will be unheard of in a few months hence. We live in an age of kaleidoscopic changes. When, however, the public has found something of resl import, which has proved an invaluable aid in the hundred minor aspects of daily life, such things are cherished and passed from family to family with the greatest regard. BAND OF HOPE MEETING AT THE PARR HALL.

Licensing Bill Supported.

Sunday last was observed as Temperance Sunday at several places of worship in Warrington and the War- rington Band of Hope Union held a public meeting in the Parr Hall in the evening. There was a good attendance, Mrs. James Dodds (president of the Union) presiding. The principal speakers were :—Mr. Alex. Thompson, of Manchester, and Mr. E. Boreland, of Liverpool (both of the United Kingdom Alliance) and amongst others on the platform were :—the Revs. J. B. Buglass, F. Vaughan, R. E. Letheren and J. L. Milne, Mrs. Simpson, itlessrs. E. J. Guest, J. Griffiths, J. Wardmao, G. Leach, W. FL Sherburn J. Jarman, S. Fernley, C. S. Fellows, G. Whitfield, Mr. and Mrs. Whitmore, Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Owen, Mr. F. Gandy (hon. secretary of the Uuion) and Mr. H. Woolley (hon. assistant secretary.) The PRESIDENT said she was yelp, glad to think the people were taking such a strong interest in the Bands of Hope. It was her privilege to attend that unique and magnificent meeting in the Free Trade Hall, 1 Manchester, some weeks ago and as she listened to first 1 one splendid speaker and then another and heard them declare that they were "Baud of Hope boys" yet, she felt that if the Band of Hope Union had done nothing more than send a band of men like that into the world, it had instilled its existence. (Applause.) Mr. E. BORELAND said that this was a great age to I live in. These were fine times for temperance fanatics. (Laughter.) They had been living up in the air since February last and to-day they stood face to face with one of the biggest problems this country had ever had to solve and they were on the way to solving it. (Applause.) He thought he was justified in saying that they owed the present position of the temperance movement to the work done in the Bands of Hope during the past sixty years. The children were coming into their kingdom at list, and none too soon. (Applause.) He heard Jelin Morley say some years ago that the greatest interest this country had was peace, and he believed the greatest asset the country had to-day was the child life of the nation. If they could only bring up the children in the way they ought to go—and they were doing that wonderfully well in connection with Band of Hope work—the future generations of England would be more sober, more godly, more Christ-like than the country was to-day. They owed the present posi- tion of the temperance movement largely to the chil- dren. He believed they would not have had 'a Licensing Bill this year if it had hot been for the interest the people took in the child life of the nation, and the teaching which had been given in the Bands of Hope in years gone by. Now that teaching was being crystallized into action, and the conscience of the nation was speak- ing out, demanding that Acts of Parliament should be passed making it easier for the people to do right and more difficult for them to do wrong. Had they made much progress during tha last fifty or sixty years? They bad, and it was not necessary to appeal to their friends only to prove it. They could appeal to the enemy. in a recent issue of" The Licensing World " there appeared an article criticising the annual report of the Commissioners of Inland Revenue for the year ended 31st March, 1908. That report gave wonderful figures and from a temperance paint of view was in every way satisfactory—in fact, "The Licensing World" said the figures pointed to the fact that we were teedOming a more sober nation. We were ; there was no doubt about that. (Applause.) In the year 1399- :900 we consumed 32 gallons of beer per head of the population ; now we conserned 27.63 gallons—nearly 41 gallons per head less. (Applause.) Eight years ago we drank nearly two gallons of spirits per head of the population ; now we consumed less than one gallon per head. It was often said that closing public-houses did not mean a reduction in the consumption of drink or in tbe volume of drunken- ness. In the ten years ending 1907 no fewer than i 16,000 liquor shops were .closed n the United Kingdom and he claimed that the figures he had just quoted were an evidence of cause and effect. What would England and Wales be at the end of fourteen years from now, when another 32,000 public-houses had gone where they , ought to have been long since? (Applause.) The fact that the conditions had so improved and that the people were asking for the passing raf a Licensing Bill was an evidence of the improved conscience of the people. (Applause.) In the city of Liverpool over GOO puhligs houses had been closed in seventeen years. The year , before the reduction began there were 14,000 convic- tions for drunkenness. Twelve years later, when they , had closed 339 liquor shops, the convictions went down to about 5,000. He admitted they had gone up since, but that was not because the public-houses were closed but because of the 1902 Act authorising policemen to arrest for simple drunkenness in a public place, as they had never done before. Besides, the population of Liverpool to-day was well nigh double what it was twenty years ago. Mr. Arthur Chamberlain told them that the Birmingham magistrates paid special attention to four districts and diminished the number of pnblic- houses in them from 115 to 54. In the same districts the convictions for drunkenness went down by 32 per cent. In 1902 an Act was passed forbidding the grant- ing of any new licences in Ireland for five years. The result vas that 1400 old licences had died a natural death and there were 14,000 fewer convictions for drunkenness. The cost of prisons in Ireland had also been reduced in the same period by the handsome sum of £9,000 a year. The experience was the same every- where. Public-houses were places of temptation to some people and when they removed the temptation they diminished the number of 'people who fell and necessarily diminished the volume of drunkenness and carried on a glorious work on behalf of sobriety, morality and prosperity. He believed the Bill now before the House of Commons would pass into law this year. (Applause.) Some of his friends talked as if the House of Lords would throw it out, but ha was of a different opinion. He did not forget that the House of Lords passed in 1853 the Scottish Sunday Closing Act ; in 1,572 they passed the hest licensing law on the. statute book of England—the 1 •vv under which we carried on the trade at the present time ; in 1878 they tattooed the Trish Sunday Closing Act ; in 1881, the Welsh Sunday Closing Act ; in 1901 the Children's Act; in 1902, the Act giving the magistrates greater control over licensed premises; in 1901 an Act laying down two good principles, with many bad ones,—that wherever compensation was eiven to the trade, the trade should find the cash and that with regard to new licences the monopoly should go to the State. He did not forget that the previous Thursday evening there passed through the committee of the House of Lords another Children's Charter for the protection of children from , many another evil. Looking at the House of Lords with ; all that history behind them,he could not help believing that they were still human beings—some of them very human--(laughter)—and that we would get greater good from them by patting them on the hack and acknowledging that they were not bad fellows after all then we would by doing the opposite. Mr. Bore- land advised the presenting of petitions to the House of Lords and also suggested that every Conservative ten)perance man should write to Lord Lensdowne some- thing like this : "My lord. I have been a Tory all my life. lam also a strong temperance man. I want this Licensing Bill to pass and if you don't let it pass, I will never vote Tory again." (Laughter end applause.) Mr. ALEX THOMPSON said they were fighting this battle for the sake of their country. It was because they loved their land that they were carrying on this great warfare against a vested interest and a selfish, greedy, grasping monopoly. (Applause.) There was" need to-day of a fine, courageous, genuine love of country which would show itself not in brag and bluster but in the desire to build up a sober Christian people, men and women strong in body, clean in mind and pure in heart. That was the great work they had set them- selves to do, and until it was done they would not rest. (Applause.) Who were the two contending parties ? On the one side they had men who were fighting for their financial interest and that alone; on the other they had men who were fighting for the interests of the country as a whole, and for the progress of the Kingdom of God. The one great reason why they supported the Government Licensing Bill was not because it was a party measure, but because it was a great patriotic endeavour to free the nation from the domination of drink. (Applause.) There was one clause of the Bill which should rejoice the hearts of all lovers of their country, and especially of those who recognized the importance of child life—the clause which provided that in future no child under fourteen years of age should be allowed within the drinking bars of our public-houses. (Applause.) That was a most important and vital thinz. They saw for the first time in its history the nation had declared though the House of Commons that the, public-house was not a safe place for the childhood of our land. It was high time such a declaration was made. They knew the revelations which George R. Sin made on that very point with regard to the public-hoases in London—the horrible and hideous descriptic Ls, be gave of many of the low public- houses. Women crowded into them; men smoked until the atmosphere was thick with tobacco; little children were bronght there and kept for hours in the vitiated atmosphere and then taken into the cold night air with little clothing on. Some mothers actually gave their infants gin. The whole country was shocked by those terr;btc sevslations, and, what was done in London was dose to i,gnate r or less extent in all our towns and great cities. At the request of the Home Office twenty- foci public-houses in Manchester were watched fc,r 96 hours and during that time nearly 5,000 -children under the age of fourteen went; into them. It was time something was done to stop that. (Applause.) When parents would not do their duty the State must step in and protect the children. (Applause) Tn 1906 over 40,000 cases were dealt with by the N,S.P.C.O , involving a very much larger number of children, and Mr. Parr, a director of the Society, stated that 90 per cent, of those cases were due to drink. There could be no greater argument why they should fight until this national degradation was removed, until the terrible foe of dsink was conquered, than the suffer- ing of thousands of little children. They must strive to inculcate temperance principles in the rising genera.

FEARED SHE WOULD FAINT. Her state of Acute Antemia cured by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills.

Every gentlewoman in Society regards the occasion of her attending at Court as one of the most pleasurable yet, nerve- racking occasions in her career. An interesting description of the ordeal was kindly given by Miss THERESA SIISItIDIN recently, when she referred to her attendance at Court. Miss Sheridan is well-known as the daughter of the late Henry Brinsley Sheridan, M.P. for Dudley for many years, whose name is revered by all smokers, he being instru- mental in securing the privilege they now enjoy smoking compartments on all railway trains. When sins kindly granted an inter- view to a London journaliat at her residence, Welbeck Court, Kensington, Miss Sheridan presented a perfect type of the healthy handsome, English lady of society, but it was quickly made apparent by her remarks that for some time until lately her health had caused her and the members of her family serious misgivings, Miss Sheridan's opening words were significant. "flow I succeeded in not fainting when I went to Court I hardly know now. A somewhat severe attack of Influenza," she mentioned, " had left me very weak and nervous. I began to have the most depressing feelings of lassitude, so that I, who had previously been of most active habits, felt unable to fulfil even ordinary engagements. Persistent headaches completely pros- trated me. Of course, I consulted medical specialists, and then learnt to my dismay that I was suffering from Anaemia. _ _

"I fear I cannot describe to you the various stages of Anmmia through which I passed. My social obligations had to be neglected, for I felt so depressed and melancholy that I could take no interest in matters of even pressing urgency. My strength entirely failed, and the slightest exertion caused me to collapse through exhaustion. My nerves were so affected that at the time when I went to Ccurt I was terribly depressed and physically ill. As the best medical treatment seemed unable to help me I went to the Riviera for some time. One would naturally expect even the most afflicted anwtnic to recover in such genial surroundings. But such was not the case. Medical attendants did their best for me ; still I could trace no improve- ment. The headaches continued and the weariness and exhaustion never left me, while I lost all my usual colour end complexion. I returned to London ill and unhappy. "Some time after this, while glancing through a periodical I read about Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People and resolved to try them, as they appeared to be so generally recommended for Anmrnia. COMPLETELY CURED.

SUFFERED WITH KIDNEY TROUBLE FOR TWELVE YEARS, AND CURED BY DR. MORSE'S INDIAN ROOT PILLS.

"I had always enjoyed good health up to the time I was 21," writes Mr. A. Sampson,. 7, Jubilee Street, Bedminster, Bristol, " when I had a severe attack of Scarlet Fever, which left me with kidney trouble. I suffered from this complaint for 12 years. About three years ago Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills were brought under my notice, and I was recommended to try them.. I gave the Pills a fair trial, and I anl now completely cured. I am a bricklayer by trade, which necessitated me stooping a great deal ; this no doubt aggravated the disease ; but at the present time I am enjoying perfect health. brought about, I am certain, by Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills. ROBUST FOOTBALLER

—(laughter)—in the last of the 70's and the early SO's when they endeavoured to distinguish themselves in that game of sport. It had been his privilege and his pleasure to meet him in many other spheres, and in all of them he had done very well and had given of his best, and left a mark in the work he had under- taken, which had proved a very great success. As an aldermae of the borough he had earned the approbation and goodwill of the members of the Council. and as Mayor he felt sure that he would earn the approbation and goodwill of the inhabitants generally. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Bennett had said that Alderman Forshaw was the first solicitor who had been made Mayor since Mr. Beamont held office, and he held Lim up as a pattern, but it was a far cry to the first Mayor. (Hear, bear.) He (Alderman Tinnion) should be perfectly satisfied if the new Mayor would remember and hold up the retiring Mayor as his pattern. (Hear, hear.) lie (the speaker) had been more than proud of the part he took in asking Alderman Smethurst to become Mayor, and events had more than justified their hopes and expectations of him—(hear, hear)—and he hoped that when they met in that room 12 months hence Alderman Forshaw would have earned the same goodwill and confidence that the late Mayor had earned. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He knew Mrs. Forshaw even before she had the pleasure of making Alderman Forshaw's acquaintance, and she had always been a good- natured soul, reedy to enter into the good work done by all charitable associations and social organizations. (Applause.) He was sure the Mayor would receive the loyal support of every member of tbe Council in dis- charging the duties of his high office. They had now lost the feeling that there was a possibility of the Council meetings being held sander disagreeable circum- stances. That feeling had, he hoped, gone for e7er. (hear, hear.) Ste hoped that all the members of the Council would do what they could to promote and keep I alive the good feeling that now existed. (Applause.) Aldermen Bennett had foreshadowed a great deal of work which he hoped the Mayor would take in bend. He trusted that during the next year they would achieve some of their great schemes, and that there would not be so much vacillation and putting off as there hnd been during the past year. (Hear, hear, and applause). The resolution was then carried unanimously and with acclammation. The ex-Mayor vacated the chair and conducted the newly elected Mayor, accompanied by Alderman Bennett and Alderman Tinnion,to the Mayor's parlour. Shortly afterwards the Mayor returned wearing his robes and chain of office and was greeted with applause.

THE MAYOR'S SPEECH. IMPORTANT SCHEMES ON HAND.

Having made the usual declarations the MA.vor. said :—When the heart is full it is difficult with the mouth to speak, and you can quite understand that my heart is full to-day. I thank you Alderman Bennett and Alderman Tinnion for the kind remarks you have made about ray wife and myself. Your references and allusions, Mr. Alderman Bennett, to our early youth- hood are perfectly true, and Imust say that as a young man I had the ambition to become (at some future time) the Mayor of my native town, believing as I do that to be of use ought to he the great aim of life. Gentlemen of the Council, I thank you sincerely for the high honour you have this day conferred upon me by electing me the Mayor and chief magistrate of my native town. It, has not been my lot to leave ray native sod, and you know, gentlemen, it is said that " A prophet is not without honour save in his own country," but you have conferred upon me the highest honour it is possible to confer upon a fellow-townsmen which goes to show what perseverance and industry can do. (Applause.) I will say at once that during my term of office I shall

KNOW NO POLITICS, but I will endeavour to fulfil the responsible ditties of this office, to the best of my ability, with impartiality, without fear or favour, and I hope with dignity and urbanity. (Applause.) I know full well that Alderman Smethurst will be a difficult man to follow, but he has kindly promised me his support and assistance, and I shall certainly take advantage of that promise. I hope you will forgive any shortcomings on my part, but I promise you that I will do my best and endeavour to do what is right. With regard to Mrs. Forshaw, I may say that as a wife she has been a splendid helpmate to me, and I am sure that, as the Mayoress of this her native town, she will prove a splendid helpmate. She will certainly do her best. (Applause.) We shall have several important matters to deal with during the ensuing year and I think the most important legacy which is left to me is

But a chairman ought to have the opportunity of retaining the positions in which he was most deeply interested. He (Alderman Bennett) would not retain the chair- manship of the Finance Committee if he had to give up a position, because his heart was in the work of the Street Improvement and Museum Committee' and he was sure there were members of the Finance Committee who could do the work as well as ho could. Ile thought every Committee ought to have full freedom to chose the man who was to preside over their deliberations. The now Standing Order was not really democratic. Councillor NIELD a chairman would deserve well of the town if he devoted himself to weighing all the details of the one department. A member who was chairmau of two or three departments was overloading himself, and there was always the possibility of a sus- picion arising that everything might not be going all right, however energetic, careful and inti:uate he might be.

TIIE BUILDING OF A NEV MUDGE

across the Mersey' I may at once say that I entirely agree with the ex-ga.yor that it is now no use attempt- ing to tinker with the existing bridge, and that the soundest and wisest policy is to go in for a new and substantial one. This I intend to bring before the Council at the earliest possible date, and I hope the Council will give it that consideration which it undoubtedly deserves. Above all let us make the bridge wide enough for the ever increasing traffic:ln my opinion SO feet is little enough. I think it will largely depend upon the term of years allowed by the Local Govern- ment Board for the repayment of the loan whether the Council build an iron bridge or a ferro-concrete bridge.

The less cost of maintenance of the latter construction may, however, counterbalsame the shorter period allowed for the repayment of the loan. I am also in entire sympathy with the ex-Mayor in his desire to construct a temporary footbridge across the Mersey with the object of opening out Victoria Park as a huge play- ground for the children of all parts of the town. The completion of Bridge-street has shown us the desirability of pushing on with the

===== 0002601_19081114_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDENING OF THE OTHER MAIN STREETS,

and as you are aware an application is now before the Local Government Board for a Provisional Order to enable us to acquire the necessary buildings and land for the widening of the streets. I hope during my Mayoralty considerable progress will be made towards those improvements. Every year Parliament imposes fresh duties on the municipalities, and it will be your and my duty this year to consider and decide upon the [ applications for old-age pensions, and I hope a wide and liberal view will be taken in the consideration of those claims which mean so much in the way of comfort and relief to those in declining years. The Council has also had imposed upon it during the year the duty of collecting local taxation licences,dogs, guns, carriages, and the like,the majority of the duties from which will be paid direct into the local exchequer instead of being received and paid through the Local Government Board as heretofore. I would, however, venture to suggest the advisability of all persons interested in the borough taking out their licences within the borough, so that we can secure the duties. You know that I have taken a great interest in providing temporary

===== 0002601_19081114_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
A LLOTAT ENTS

for the use of the working class, and T hope the Council will assist me in utilizing any other lands belonging to the Corporation for the provision of further allotments. The land in Victoria Park appropriated for that purpose will not be required probably for many years for park purposes. Therefore I think we are making the best use of the land in the meantime, especially when we consider that it is being done without loss to the town. I hope some progress will be made in the laying out of the rest of Victoria Park during the year. (Applause.) I regret to find that there are so many people out of employment, but I am glad to think that the distress is not likely to be so great as in many other industrial towns of Lancashire. I regard it as the duty of the Town Council to at once consider what works of public utility can be put.in hand to provide

WORK FOR TRE UNEMPLOYED. One cannot help feeling for the poor women and chil- dren who suffer so severely for the want of food and warmth in the bitter winter months. I am happy to think that with the exception of one or two trades, work in the town is improvirm, and I shall be glad if the employers of labour will make the fullest use of our labour bureau, which is so excellently carried on by our local Distress Committee. In conclusion, if T have, as I hope to have, your support ond co-operation I feel sure that I shall succeed, and I hope give satisfaction carryinz out the responsible and onerous duties that will devolve upon me as Mayor and Chief Magistrate of this important county borough. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19081114_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPUTY I‘IAYOII.

The MAyort then appointed Alderman Smethurst to act as his deputy. Alderman Smurlrutts'r in returning thanks said he hoped that during his term of office he had not sullied a single link of the Mayoral chain and he was sure that in Alderman Forshaw's keeping the chain would lose none of its brightness. (Applause.) They could not expect a man like Alderman Forshaw, who hgd a busi- ness to conduct,to devote as much time to the duties of the Mayoralty as one who had nothing else to do. Mrs. Smethurst and himself would therefore do what they could to help the Mayor and Mayoress during the next twelve months, and any service they could render would be given with a willing heart. (Applause).

11IAYOR'S CIIAPLATN. The Mayor then appointed the Rev. C. C. Briggs as his chapl lin, and announce l that it was his inten- tion to go to the Parish Church on Sunday morning, and he should be glad if as many townsmen as possible would accompany him there. In the evening he would go unofficially to the blue Coat School Chapel.

IMPORT- ANT NEW STANDING ORDER. HONOURS TO GO ROUND.

The Council having fixed the usual dates for the hold- ing of rmeting,s, tho MAYOR proposed, and Alderman SMETHURST seconded, the adoption of the Standing Orders. _

Alderman T/NNION called attention to a new Standing Order, which prevented any member of the Council being chairman or deputy-chairman of more than two committees of the Council, or acting as their representa- tive on more than two public bodies. He said that in theory that might be very good, but it should ho the duty and privilege of every committee to appoint its own chairman and deputy-chairman. Four gentlemen were affected by the new Standing Order, which was going to be to the detriment of the Corporation. Alderman Bennett would have to give up his position as chairman of'the Finance Committec, and if anybody was fitted for that position it was a professional accountant. Then it would be a loss and great incon- venience if Alderman Evans decided to give up the chairmanship of the Sanitary Works Committee. There was no old member on that committee who could under- take the work. Alderman Smethurst would not be able to take the chair ns deputy-chairman. Neither could be (Alderman Tinnion), though the new Order did not affect him, as he was chairman of only two committees. The chairmanship and deputy-chairmanship of the Sanitary Works Committee would have to be in the hands of two junior members. He moved that the Order be deleted. _

Aldbrraan SMETHURST soconded and said if passed the new order would entirely disorganise the work of the Council. Alderman Evans had re-modelled the Sanitary Works and it ,would he a pity to remove him when further alterations were in haad. As 'far as he (Alder- ran Smethurst) was concerned ho would fall in with the idea, and he would retire from the deputy-chairman- ship of the Sanitary Works Committee.

PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica.

During the past few weeks I have several times referred to various aspects of winter photography. At the moment, I wish to speak rather of the general sub- ject, and hope to follow these remarks with more particularised and practical details in succeeding columns. The number of new recruits in photography has been very great during the past year or two, and writers are apt to forget that their readers include many who need to be informed of some of the basic facts. Of course, all old photographers know that the winter-time can be as busy and profitable as the summer, but many new beginners are apt to lay away their cameras as SOOS RS the trees shed their leaves.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
NEGATIVE-MAKING IN WINTER.

The possibilities of negative-making are largely cur- tailed by the fact that most photographers can get very little daylight time in winter, and the result of this should be to make them turn their attention, as far as possible, to subjects in or near their homes. This means that portraits (including studies of animals and other pets), still life nod interiors should have a special turn. I showed a week or two ago that much of this work can be done by means of ordinary gas or paraffin lamps, and last winter about this time, I dealt at some - length with the use of magnesium wire for interior work. During the daylight hours, too, much work can be done almost as well in the winter as in the summer, simply increasing the exposure for these interior subjects to two or four times what would be given' under similar circumstances in the height of summer. EVENING WORK. _ . I

..... I The present-day photographer is greatly favoured in having the chance of using gaslight and bromide papers, and he is to be particularly congratulated on the fact that the gaslights and slow contact papers together with the slow lantern plates, can be worked quite conveniently in an ordinary living room with the usual evening lighting. COPYING AND ENLANGING. .

The maki;ii-or enirtr;en7en-W-10-r-fi;;Mit-ig, and the copying of pictures, prints' music,. and other objects, forms interesting and useful work for the winter. The , common ideas as to the need of extensive and expensive' ' apparatus for this purpose is very largely based on mistake, for while a special apparatus is very cion--, venient when a man is doing much work, it is not at all necessary when he does only occasional jobs. CHRISTMAS PRESENTS. Te making of prints and their suitable mounting or framing, especially if they are finished for Christmas cards or albums, enables the photographer to turn his hobby to great advantage in the winter time, and to give presents which will be much more appreciated than those which are bought at the shops, and are just the same as may be given by, and to other people. THE KNOW-HOW.' Another very great advantage of the photographer of to-day is that in addition to the simplifications of all his methods, there has been provided a very complete series of practicable and easily understandable text books, issued by various publishers, and supplied at very reasonable prices. The most extensive series is the Photo-Miniature," which now deals with no loss than ninety-two subjects, and each book of which is sold at the reasonable price of Gd. Many other honks- at Gd. or Is. are also issued, so that the amateur who will call call at his dealers shop or write to one of the publishers for a complete catalogue, will find it easy to take up any new branch of photography that may attract ' him. THE TRAILL-TAYLOR LECTURE. Some of the newer generation of photographers will probably feel some wonder when they see the announce— ment of the 11th Traill-Taylor Memorial Lecture, which is to be given at the Royal Photographic Society on November 17th, for there are many photographers who knew nothing of the art-science in the days when J. Traill-Taylor was a great power in the land, lie was indeed one of the " giants ' in those days, and held a position such as is not occupied by anyone at the present time. For many years he was editor of "The British Journal of Photography, and had a good deal to do with raising it to its unique position of influence. When9ie died a subscription was made for the sake of establishing a memorial, some hundreds of pounds were raised, and it was decided to establish a lecture-ship, awl-ding a large handsome bronze Traill-Taylor medal each year to some important man who would give an original lecture on photography or the kindred sciences, preferably on the optical side, in which Mr. Train- Taylor was specially interested. The lecture this year is to be by Dr. E. ‘Vanderstob, on " The Regulation of the Ray in a Lens System." Anyone who is in London, and is Interested can obtain a ticket by applying to the Secretary of the Royal Photographic Society, by which organisation the Trai 11-Taylor Fund is now administered.

EXHIBITION RECORD-3. Tn spite of the competing attractions of the fine weather and the Franco-British Exhibition, the number of visitors to the Royal Photographic Society's Exhibi- tion at the new Gallery, was slightly in advance of the number admitted in any previous normal year. Last year was quite abnormal because of the novelty of the autochrome process led to the attendance of large numbers of people who were not interested in photo- graphy generally, but who were attacted by a novelty. The highest previous record was 15,221 admissions in 1900, and the number this year amounted to 15,621 excluding those nho attended the private view and soiree, which would have raised the total ta well over 18,003.

Evidently members of PARLIAMENT'S Parliament are not greatly "IRUINED, disturbed by the ;continued QUIRES." exclusion of strangers. Mr. WILL THORNE, it is true, is anxious to see the ladies back behind the Grille, but he does not seem to be eagerly supported, even by the members of the Labour party. Here we have Parliament conducting its business every day only in the presence of the Press and occasionally of two or three Peers, and yet the heavens do not fall I It seems to be accepted a, the fact that in the House of Commons, as at executions, the Press is a sufficient representation of the public. The Speaker has been making inquiries into the private sentiments of mem- bers on both sides of the House, and has come to the conclusion that the closing of the galleries is not the subject of serious complaint anywhere, The matter is entirely -within his own discretion. unless he should receive some definite instruction from the House. Should he be so minded, there- fore, he may keep the galleries closed so long as he occupies the chair. Members would thus be saved from what has been a permanent source of worry. They have been harrassed every day by constituents who desired to get a seat in one of the galleries for themselves or their friends. They have had to ballot for places in the Ladies'Gallery, on the chance of being successful once in a blue moon, and all at once this onerous responsibility has been lifted from their shoulders, and if the Speaker is to be believed (as, of course, he must be), they are rejoicing at their new-found freedom. All attacks on Parliamentary quiet have the same effect of restricting public privileges. Until an attempt was made to blow up Westminster Hall it was not difficult for any enterprising stranger to get into the members' lobby. Indeed, that portion of the Parliamentary precincts was not much less accessible than the central hall or outer lobby is at the present day. Admission to it represented an ancient, popular right, namely, the right of access of the constituent to his representative, corresponding to the right of the nobility to approach the occupant of the Throne. But when the explosion took place the lobby was closed to everybody, Press included, and the public has never been re-admitted and the Press only to a carefully restricted extent and on special conditions. A member may take a stranger to the lobby for the brief space of three minutes, and that is all that remains of an ancient privilege. He might, during the permitted three minutes, let a lady peep at the House of Commons through a small window. This also is a privilege that has been withdrawn, and one that is very unlikely to be restored. Thus it is that the more daring enthusiasts limit the opportunities of ordinary folk. The existing situation has led Mr. WILLIE REDMOND to make a really sensible suggestion. It is that opportunity should now be taken of increasing the restricted accommodation of the Press. Mr. REDMOND, obviously, is of opinion that some portion of the limited space to which the public has hitherto been admitted should be handed over to the newspapers. We are not selfish enough to second him in this part of his pro- position, if that is what he really means, but otherwise his recommendation is a distinctly good one. The accommodation provided for the Press in the House of Commons is much more liberal than it used to be, but but it is still grotesquely insufficient, and is besides at the mercy of a

/ system which is thoroughly bad, and which makes distinctions for which there is no ground whatever in commoasense or in common fairness. But as the public is certain to go back some day, we should not be disposed to cramp it up more than was formerly the case. We would take the extra space required not from the Strangers' Gallery, nor even from the Grille, but from members of Parliament. Precisely on a level with the Press Gallery there are on each side of the House two rows of seats which are only occu- pied on the occurrence oi great debates. They are reserved for members who cannot find space on the floor of the House. Oa all but special occasions they are like " bare, ruined quires, in which tbe sweet birds sang." Or they would be like that if they had ever been used for vocal purposes. Mr. WILLIE REDUOND might turn his attention to these.

The election of Alderman THE NEW MAYOR. Forshasv to the Mayoralty of Warrington provides one of those rare and interesting incidents of a man commencing in the humblest sphere and rising to the highest office in his native town. It is no secret' that His Worship of to-day was once a Blue Coat boy, and like a proper man he is proud of the fact. Curiously enough his employer as a a youth—the late Alderman Joseph Davies—was likewise a Blue Coat boy anc' he also rose to be Mayor of Warrington. The old Charity School would have had the distinction of supplying three Mayors of Warrington if the honour had also been conferred on Mr. F. Monks, as it should have been years ago. We have no doubt that the present occupant of the Mayoral chair will honour both his office and his school. It has been one of his ambitions in life—as he told the cordial assembly which saw him invested with the robes of office on Monday—to become Mayor of his native town, and doubtless that ambition has been the spur to Alderman Forshaw's unflagging industry and perseverance. The new Mayor showed an admirable appreciation of the dignity and responsibilities of his office in his inaugural speech, and we noted with pleasure his emphatic declaration that as Mayor he would "know no politics." Moreover he expressed in words a deep sympathy with the poor and needy which we have no doubt he will demonstrate in deeds during his term of office.

There were interesting FREE CHURCHMEN references at the annual AND RELIGIOUS meeting of the Warrington EDUCATION. Free Church Council on

Thursday to the Education question. The Rev. R. Rainey Workman, of Manchester, took up a determined attitude and said there must be no compromise: "Sectarianism must go; no longer shall it live upon the rates." The only solution he favoured was the secular one. Mr. Workman thought that religious education should be imparted in the Sunday schools, and if necessary he was in favour of paying the Sunday school teacher as well as the parson. The Rev. J. B. Buglass dissented from this uncompromising ' attitude, and expressed his belief in an early and reasonable settlement based on mutual concessions by the contending factions. Personally, we agree entirely with the Rev. Rainey Workman. A compromise is doubtless possible, but that it will bring about a settlement of the question, and lay the bogey of religious education, we cannot conceive. Sectarianism will prove too strong in the end for any compromise, and the sooner Free Churchmen face the drastic but sound and logical alternative of secular education, and the better for the children and for the nation. If Churchmen doubt their capacity to give adequate religious teaching in their own Sunday schools at their own expense, there should be no hesitancy by Free Churchmen in accepting the responsibility for their own part. Nonconformity has only reached its present high position by faith and self•reliance. Is it to be found lacking now in either? We trow not!

We are astonished to find THE ROAD T 3 that 111 r. Robert Pierpoint HEAVEN. —a man who has had

sufficient experience of life to make most people broadminded—actually believes that the Church of Rigland owns the only road to Heaven, and that without a pass- port from one of the priests of that Church the dying sinner has precious little chance of reaching Paradise. This is the only construction we can put on his remarks at the opening of St. Peter's Sale of Work last week. He is reported as saying: He (Mr. Pierpoint) should be inclined to say that generally speaking, when the great events of the individual life happened, that was when a man and a woman got married, or when either was about to die, they were more apt to send for a clergyman of the Church of England than for the minister of any other denomination. What he meant to say was that even those who were not in Its habit of attending a church but chapel, when , the great occasions of life came, preferred the I office in many cases of the Church. (blear, hear, and applause.) The italics are ours but we do not know who was responsible for the " hear hear and applause." Evidently Mr. Pierpoint was not alone in his belief. But what a thing to say ! Mr. Pierpoint has been a Member of Parliament—for the last time—and he has mixed there with people of all denominations on terms of equality. We presuma he would be one of the first to insist that the House of Commons is a Christian assembly, and per- haps he would also admit that there are Chris- tians on both sides of the House. But if we are to take what he said at St. Peter's as the dictum of an ordered and balanced mind we &re to imagine that the ex-Member for War- rington believes that when they are face to face with Death and tha last moments are nigh, those Nonconformist M.P.'s " opposite " who have been such stalwarts of their own faith through this life will cravenly send for a clergyman of the Established Church to pilot them through the Valley of the Shadow to the Glory beyond. Mr. Pierpoint's statement is an insult to every member of the Dissenting Churches. It suggests that not only is their religion a poor and graceless thing, but that they will readily abandon it in order to receive the last rites of the Established Church. We never heard or read a more amazing allegation, born even of religious bigotry, and we hope the Nonconformists of Warrington will take the trouble to impress Mr. Robert Pierpoint with the enormity of his oftence.

The members of the A TESTIMONY go Warrington Paving and WARRINGTON Sewerage Committee have PAYING, reason occasionally, to blush

to find themselvs-s. famous. Nearly every tourist, on foot or cycle,who samples the Warrington paving finds therein a subject on which be can express himself freely and forcibly. During the past fortnight Mr. George Allen, a famous pedestrian, has been describing his record walk from Land's End to John O'Groats, in "Health and Strength," and the current number contains an account of the tramp from Tarporley to Lancaster. After expatiating on the beauties of Cheshire, Mr. Allen records his impressions on approaching and passing through Warrington: "'Trade's impelling train' had indeedj usurped the land, for Warrington, the town of smoke and rough road setts, was just ahead. The pavement here is a disgrace to ,the town, and as I passed through I wondered how long it would be before the authorities would come into line with other up-to-date towns in this respect, and put down something that made it possible to enjoy a walk in town. By mid-day we were well out of Warrington and hurrying our steps to reach and leave Wigan behind too." This is by no means the first unsolicited testi- monial to Warrington pavements from competent judges, and it ought to make our Paving and Sewerage Committee to think furiously. We have long growled in similar tones. Warrington is certainly the worst paved town of its size that we have ever come across. The two main roads through Latch- ford and Wilderspool, and onward through Loogferd, are more than a disgrace—they are a

scandal to the manicipality.There are holes and ruts inches deep, and tramlines that project above the road-surface like kerb-stones. The crown of Black Bear Bridge need only be taken as a sample. If there is a worse section of pavemlnt on a main road anywhere we should like to see it and have it photographed. It would make an Etrly Roman blush. Better roads were made before civilization had dawned. And yet Warrington is described as a progressive municipality

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[FROM OEM LONDON CORRESPONDENT.]

An international situation charged with momentous possibilities made the occasion of the Prime Minister's Guildhall speech, in which, in accordance with a long- standing custom, he passed in survey our relations with the rest of the world, more than usually notable and impressive. And the speech, it has been admitted on all hands, was worthy of a high occasion. It was especially distinguished by that rare combina- tion of frankness with restraint which is the note of true statesmanship. The keenest critics of the Government's domestic legislation are ready now to acknowledge that the course of this country's external I policy, through a crisis of the utmost gravity, is being shaped by strong and skilful hands. how strange to-day appears what used, a few years ago, to be the standing plea of the Conservative Party, that whatever might be the shortcomings of a Conservative Govern. ment,it ought to be maintained in power, as only on that condition had we any guarantee that the foreign affairs of this country would be properly administered. Besides the tense expectancy which passing events in Europe have created, the business that has been transacting in Parliament seems very humdrum. The Committee stage of any Bill is apt to prove very laborious, if not dull. But an extended and complex measure like the LicenQing Bill involves untold labour to those who have to pilot it through Committee. Mr. Herbert Samuel, who was heartily congratulated from the Opposition benches on his elevation to the Privy Council, and the Solicitor-General thoroughly deserved the compliment paid by their opponents to the way in which they had conducted the Bill. The consideration of the Scottish Education and the Port of London Bills followed in the interval before the Report stage of the Licensing Bill. Mr. R.unciman laid before the Nonconformist members the terms of a compromise which was understood to have the approval of the Archbishop of Canterbury, and a resolution was passed by an overwhelming majority of the Nonconformist members promising support to the Government action on these lines. GREAT BRITAIN'S ATTITUDE DEFINED.

It would be futile to attempt to condense into a brief summary the wide range of international politics over which Mr. Asquith took his Guildhall audience. But the general effect was to define the attitude of the British Government towards three great questions : the re-affirmation of the public law of Europe, after its disturbance by recent events in the East; the rela- tions between the Great Powers, not only as affected by the Eastern crisis, but in their larger aspects • and the conditions and requirements of national defence. On the one hand, the Prime Minister was addressing himself to Continental opinion, and clearing away sus- picions and misunderstandings that have been born of idle rumour; on the other, he was administering a caution to public opinion at home against a harmful kind of speech and thought sometimes in vogue regarding the aims and ends that diplomacy ought to pursue, and he was giving the most weighty and explicit assurance that the supremacy of our Navy is ' being, and, while the present Government is in power, will always be fully maintained.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
AN UNSELFISH POLICY.

The Prime Minister has defined the attitude of Great Britain to the problems of the Near East with a precision that leaves no room for mistake. British policy has three unambiguous objects ; the assertion of public law in Europe, fair play for the new regime in Turkey, and such a readjustment as will afford adequate security for future peace. The British Government insists that to give validity to the altera- tions of the Berlin Treaty effected by what has taken place in Bulgaria, Bosnia, and Herzegovina, they must obtain the assent of the Powers, including Turkey, which are parties to that Treaty. But the Prime Minister stated emphatically that the British Government has no prejudice against or preference for any particular method by which a settlement may be reached. There has never, he declared, been even the shadow of a foundation for the suggestion that Great Britain has deprecated or discouraged direct negotiations between Turkey and Austria or Turkey and Bulgaria. The alterations of the Treaty of Berlin must, to have any , validity, receive the consent of all the parties to the Treaty. sre have taken our stand on the principle affirmed by the Powers at the London Conference of 1871, that international treaties cannot be altered by the act of one of the parties to them without the con- sent of all the rest. But if Turkey, which is the chief sufferer by the alterations, can arrive at a direct understanding with Bulgaria or A ustria, the way will be smoothed for a general settlement.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
NO ISOLATION OR HOSTILE GROUPING.

One of the happiest results of the Anglo-Russian agreement has been the frank and sympathetic discus- sion between the two contracting parties of the crisis which has arisen in the Near East, and they have been able to approach it from the same point of view. In the dissensions that are distracting Persia, Great Britain and Russia have been able to act together with the same harmony. But the Prime Minister addressed a warning to public opinion at home and an assurance to those Powers who might be in danger of misapprehending the aims of British policy, that this Government is as far as possible from wishing to see Europe divided into groups on the Near East ques- tion. We have found ourselves in complete sympathy with France, who is an ally of Russia, but we have been as frank with Germany and Italy, who are allies of Austria, as with France and Russia. There ought to be no talk, at such a time, of hostile groupings or rival combinations. It is the firm and unalterable resolve of the whole country not to fall short in any one of the special engagements which we have undertaken. But, at the same time, as the Prime Minister said " we shall not be reluctant to grasp any hand which is extended to us in goodwill and good faith"; and in this connection Mr.Asq4ith recalled the German Emperor's emphatic declaration at the Guildhall last year that the governing principle of his policy was the preservation of the peace of Europe and the maintenance of good relations between Germany and Great Britain. THE SUPREUACY OF OUR NAVY.

Is on this mistaken idea of a British policy of isolation, so in regard to national defence' the Prime Minister was speaking to a double audience ; an audience at home and abroad. It was not necessary to remind the British nation that upon our Navy we have to place our first and chief reliance, not only for the protection of our shores, but for the protection of our commerce and the sustenance of our people. Some of our foreign critics, however, forget this when they suspect aggressive purposes behind our insistence on the supremacy of our Navy. Mr. McKenna and Mr. Asquith were both equally clear and forcible upon the vital need of keeping this supremacy unimpaired, and upon the present adequatenese of the Navy for any responsibilites that could conceivably be thrown upon it.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
-MODIFICATIONS IN THE LICENSING BILL.

The Licensing Bill has received some important modifications during its passage through Committee. Some of these were effected by amendments at the time, others have been embodied and defined in amendments placed on the paper by the Government for the Report stage. A further period of seven years during which the monopoly value will not be taken on the renewal of a licence, has been added to the period of fourteen years during which compensation will be paid on reduction. The discretion of the licensing justices and the power of a two-thirds majority of the inhabitants in every parish and urban area to exercise local option, will be unfettered during the seven years period. But it will be a period of " rest " in as far as the obligation on the licensing justices to bring reduction up to a scheduled minimum will have been carried out by the end of the fourteen years. Conse- quently, there is a certain "expectancy" of renewal during the period of " rest " for those licences which survive the fonrteen years. Therefore, in fixing com- pensation for licences extinguished during the fourteen years period, an additional three years' value is to be added. Another concession was the abandonment of the proposal that licensing justices might make it a condition of renewal—under the existing law they may make it the condition of granting a new licence —that women should not be employed in the sale of liquor. One amendment extends the proposed restriction of Sunday opening to London, but-allows four hours in London as against three in the rest of England. Other amendments for the Report stage have proposed that public-housee alma' is closed

during Parliamentary elections; that a condition of renewal of licences may prohibit the sale of intoxicat- ing liquor before 8 a.m. ; that a court of summary jurisdiction, instead of the licensing justices, shall hear the annual claims of clubs for registration, and that registration shall be refused to the tied" clubs. THE PERSPECTIVE OF THE BILL.

It is desirable, after the mass of detail which tends to cover up the broad outlines of a measure daring discussion in Committee' to try to obtain a fresh perspective of the Bill. The backbme of the measure is, of course, the provision for recovering for the community full control of the drink traffic and possession of the monopoly value, and for making an effective reduction in superfluous licensed houses. At the same time, tho regulations for closing on Sunday and during elections constitute in themselves a distinct advance in temperance reform. The regulations with regard to clubs will effectually stop a possible loop- hole by which the evils against which the legislation in regard to public-houses is aimed might break out in another form. How unreal is the criticism which Hr. Balfour, as Leader of the Opposition, has directed against the Bill appeared at once when the legislative proposals against drinking-clubs were reached. He attacked legislation against the excessive number of public-houses and the lack of public control over them, because he maintained that such legislation would only foster the growth of drinking-clubs ; and then with bland disregard of consistency, he attacked the Government's legislative proposals to prevent the existence of clubs for the mere purpose of selling intoxicating liquor. It was only too clear that he had abdicated the functions of an honest critic, and was only holding a watching brief forthe brewers.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPARATIVE GROWTH OF TRADE.

Some remarkable statistics have been issued officially, showing the comparative growth of trade in Great Britain, Germany, and the United States. It has become quite a parrot-cry of Tariff Reformers that the British export of manufactured articles is fast declining through the influence of Free Trade. Well, the growth of these exports, as shown by a com- parison between 1900 and 1907, has been : in Great Britain, -113 millions of pounds sterling ; in the United States, 50 millions; and for Germany, fif) millions. If we take the proportion per head of population, we find that Great Britain's increase has been £2 4s. 2d. per head, for the United States 10s. 11d., and for Germany .-C:1 3s. 9d.
