NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The Chinese are still steadily being repatriated, and 2,314 were sent back last month. The total number on the Rand is now only 12,317, the reduction since the high-water mark of January 1907 being 41,539. Indeed, if it had not been for the 16,000 permits for licences which were given out just before the present Government came into office there would be no Chinese coolies left at all in the mines. * * *

Old-age pensions are providing a real and greatly- appreciated boon to the poor and elderly. The War- rington Committee has passed 12,5 applications for the full pension of ss. and for several of smaller amounts. One curious point has arisen in respect of Co-operative Society dividends which the pension officer wished to reckon as income,but the local Com- mittee refused to accept this view, and the matter has been referred to the Local Government Board. Per- sonally, we think the Committee is in the right, and that it would be most unfair to penalise the thrifty co-operator. * * *

The question of the Warrington Board of Guardians' private fund account has slumbered so long that people were beginning to think that they had beard the last of it. At Wednesday's meeting, however, Mrs. Collingwood reminded the Guardians that the affair had not yet been cleared up, and plainly declared that she was not satisfied with the position as it stands at present. The Chairman apparently took alarm and hinted at going into committee, but a spirited protest by Miss Broadbent against the policy of " hushing up " appeared to convince the Board that the discus- sion ought to proceed in public. However, in the absence of full particulars, nothing further could be done, but the last has not been heard of the matter. * * *

In order to take a truly exalted view of mundane affairs it is as well to be a nobleman. Lord William Cecil has been writing to the " Times " in order to protest against " tho modern plan of employing clumsy, lower-class policemen to keep high-spirited gentlemen in order." The Oxford students have recently been indulging in the custom of letting off fireworks in the street and creating other disturbances which have resulted in police-court appearances. His Lordship plainly thinks that there should be one law for the rich and another for the poor, at any rate in Oxford, where high-spirited "young geatlemen " are prone to break it. * * *

Councillor Peacock's 'victory in Orford Ward and his bold defiance of the brewers have prompted that conglomeration of selfish interests known as the Tory party to endeavour to find some means of unseating. him. We hear that no stone has been left unturned in order to find evidence that some incautious worker for the successful candidate bribed a voter. Rumour has it that the witness has been found, but whether his word would carry weight in a Court of Justice is another matter. We also hear that the Liberals have been turning over a few stones in St. John's Ward and have made discoveries which justify them in promising that one petition will be followed by another. The game seems to be "You unseat our man, and we'll unseat yours," but it is hardly likely to be played to a finish.

** * • The cotton dispute came to an end on Friday and work began again on Monday, after a stoppage lasting seven weeks. We are very glad that the workmen have at last the good sense to recognise that they could not expect to win. Though we do nut want the settlement now arrived at treated as a victory for one party or a defeat for the other, there is no getting away from the simple fact that in order to avoid J per cent. reduction for two months the workpeople have lost seven weeks' full wages, and at the same time spent a large sum of their own accumulated funds. The resumption of work will avert what would rapidly have grown into a general industtial dislocation ía Lancashire. * * *

Possibly the most remarkable feature of Mr. Taft's career is the fact that the Presidency of the United States is practically the first representative office to which he has ever been elected or for which, indeed, he has ever been a candidate. The only previous occasion on which he has sought the votes of his fellow-citizens was in 1888, when he was chosen to continue for two years an Ohio judgeship which he had already been holding for a short time by the State Governor's appointment. He has never been a mem- ber of any legislative body, and the executive offices he has held, whether at home or in the Philipines, have all come to him by appointment, not by election. No recent President could parallel this record. Mr. Roosevelt sat for a term in the New York State Assembly, ran unsuccessfully for the Mayoralty of New York City, and was later elected to the State Governorship and the Vice-Presidency. IPKinley spent fourteen years in Congress, and was afterwards Governor of Ohio. Cleveland was holding the Governorship of New York State when nominated for the Presidency. * * *

The great tooth-brush question which has been agitating the minds of the Warrington Board of Guardians for some weeks, is at last settled. The children at the Padgate Homes are not to have tooth brushes. When the matter came before the Cottage Homes Committee at the instance of the Board, Mr. Mather, with rare humour, moved" That we proceed with the next business "and the majority of the com- mittee readily seized this way out of the difficulty. At the next meeting, however, Miss Broadbent again championed the tooth-brush cause and moved a resolu- tion that the necessary number of those useful— and one would have thought indespensable— articles be purchased. But the Committee wee obdurate and the proposal was defeated. Only a fortnight ago one enlightened Guardian predicted that a dozen cases of appendicitis would follow the introduction of tooth-brushes, and another member added that he had had appendicitis but never had a tooth-brush. These are the type of arguments which have convinced the Cottage Homes Committee that tooth-brushes are unnecessary, if not dangerous luxuries. It is no good reasoning with people of this kind, but if their teeth do not ache and drop out of their heads through neglect and deoay there is ao justice in the world.

' lady, ( - Mrs. Garrett Anderson, who has become the first Mayor in England, has been a pioneer in several fields. Her struggle for the admission of women to . the medical profession belongs to another generation in every sense of the words. It was in 1865 that the College of Surgeons and Physicians refused to admit lit*--, her to its examinations, and she was compelled five years later to obtain the licence of the Society of the Apothecaries, while her M.D. was taken in Paris. She was a suffragist, too, when the peesent generation 01 militants was unborn (adds the " Westminster Gazette "), and he has played a large part in the local affairs of Aldeburgh, of which she now becomes Mayor,

The Examiner Saturday, November 14, 1908.

A Lymm Man's Adventure. The loss of a bag of gold, with £.13, by John Scott living at Lymm, was the subject of a prosecution before the Manchester Stipendiary Magistrate on Thursday. A young man named Charles Gordon, of Dyke-street, Rochdale-road, was charged with stealing it. Scott alleged that he was in Dyke-street on November 4th, on his way to see a friend, when a woman called him into a house. Directly he got inside, the woman put her hand into his coat pocket, and relieved him of his bag, with the money it con- tained. The woman and the prisoner together the- threw him into the street and closed the door to pre vent his return. He informed a policeman,who went to the house, only to find that the woman and tho money had disappeared. Subsequently Gordon was arrested at Bury. Gordon was remanded. Backhaus Concert. • •

Warrington will be favoured with a visit on Monday, the 30th inst., of the Backhaus concert party including two world-renowned artists —Backhaus, one of the greatest pianists of the day, and Madame Ella Russell, the celebrated prima donna. Backhaus is making a tour round the world next year, and this will be his last visit to England for some considerable time. He has not only captivated London by his wonderful playing and magnetic powers, but he has a world-wide reputation. Madame Ella Russell, who now resides in Italy, is visiting England expressly for this tour, and will sing a specially attractive pro- gramme. In every branch of her art —opera,oratorio, or the concert platform —she is equally delightf Seats may be booked and tickets obtained at M. R. H. Pearson's music warehouse, 75, Bridge street.

Warrington Bridge and its History. An illustrated lecture entitled "A History of Warrington Bridge" was delivered before the mem- bers of the Warrington Literary and Philosophical Society on Monday by Mr. Charles Madeley, the director of the Museum. Ur. Madeley dealt in an interesting way with the first Warrington Bridge, the date of the erection of which is unknown but which was referred to at so early a date as 1301. A second bridge took its place about 1304 but this also had completely disappeared in 100 years from its con- struction. For more thsti 30 years there was no bridge and it seemed to be nobody's business to pro- vide one. They came now to the event which bulked so large in the traditional history of Warrington—the building of the bridge, often splken of as the first bridge, by the Earl of Derby in 1495. Mr. Madeley gave an interesting description of the old bridge and the historical scenes it witnessed, and made reference to its successors. He had no details as to the erection of the present bridge, but it was opened in Ig3i.

OLD-AGE PENSIONS.

Important Point Raised at Warrington.

A meeting of the Warrington Old Age Pensions Com- mittee was held at the Town Hall on Thursday after- noon under the presidency of the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw.) The total number of claims dealt with was NO, and the Committee granted 120 pensions of ss. each and a few of smaller amounts, and adjourned other applications for further consideration at the next meet- ing, on December Bth. In one CRSO an important point was raised. The pen.ion officer had included in the income of an appli- cant the amount received as dividend on purchases from the Co-operative Society, but the committee declined to regard such dividend as income,and awarded the applicant the full pension. Tl officer will report r the disagreement to the Local Government Board, who will decide the appeal. Applications were received from various friendly socie- ties in the town for representation on the committee. We understand that the committee having decided not to appoint sub-committees, but to undertake the duties of the Act themselves, cannot now co-opt outside mem- bers as the Act provides that the number on the Pension Committee shall not exceed the number of the appointing Council. Most of the members were strongly of opinion that they were quite capable of looking after the interests of the friendly societLis in common with those of the other inhabitants of the town.

BRASS AND COPPER HEARTH SUITES, see window display for new and artistic patterns.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. A Child's Death Mr. F. A. Jones held an inquest at Warrington on Tuesday on the body of Richard Stephen Forrest, 12 months old,son of Charles Forrest, a general labourer, living at 2, Morris-court.—The father of the child, Charles Forrest, stated that his wife was not a sober woman, and had been turned out of several houses. In September last the child was taken ill with diarrhcea and vomiting, and was removed to the Infirmary. In October witness's son was locked up for stealing, and he said that he had been sent out by his mother to steal. When remonstrated with, she went away, taking the now deceased child and another child with her. Inspector Cox said he had had the case under observa- tion for a lone' time, and he had repeatedly warned the mother as to her treatment of her child which was quite healthy when born. One of the children had told him that on Thursday last she and her mother walked all the way from Wigan. —Dr. Ilodgkinson, who had made a post-mortem examination of the body of the deceased, said he was of opinion that the child had probably been neglected, but he was not in a position to say that the neglect had hastened death. The jury returned a verdict accordingly, the foreman adding that the woman deserved to be censured for her conduct to the child. Inspector Cox: The case will be reported to the society. —The Deputy Coroner said he understood that the mother had disappeared, and that no one knew where she had gone. The father had not a penny with which to bury his child, and he had decided to allow him £2 out of the Coroner's poor box. The Late Mr. FL C. Kite.—Concert at the Parr Hall. On Saturday evening last a concert was given in the Parr Hall, under the auspius of the L. and N.-W. THE MAYOR.

Congratulations at ths Pollee Court.

I The new Mayor of Warrington (Alderman W. J. Forshaw) made his first appearance on the Bench at the ' Police Court yesterday morning, and received a warm welcome from the local solicitors who had assembled in large numbers. Mr. W. 11. J. Jenkins, on behalf of his colleagues, offered their sincere congratulations to the Mayor on the attainment of the highest horour it was in their power as citizens to bestow upon him. His training would enable him to act with right discrimin- ation, and he hoped he would be blessed with wisdom Ito hold the scales of justice with equal poise. So far his private life was concerned, they sincerely trusted that all the duties which His Worship had to perform ' might leave behind them nothing but pleasureable thoughts to himself and his family. I The Mavou thanked Mr. Jenkins very cordially for his kindly congratulations and good wishes. He took it as a great compliment that his legal brethren had ' assembled in such large numbers to offer him their con- gratulations. If he had their support, he would feel greatly encouraged in fulfilling the trying and difficult duties attaching to that important office. He promised all who practised in that Court, that as far as he was • concerned, they would have a patient and fair hearing in any oases which came before him and he would also endeavour to execute justice with mercy and to main- tain truth. In conclusion His Worship said he would , like to see more esprit de corps in the profession and he thought if a local Law Society were formed it might bring that end about. Such a Society would have his best support.

STOCKTON HEATH.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. " By—Kraiist."

TO-DAY'S MATCH WITH AUSTRALIA.

The great match with the " Kangaroos" will take place to-day and should attract a fine crowd. It will be remembered that last December the A.ll Blacks of New Zealand were here and after a great match were defeated by 8 points to 7. Warrington should have a fair chance of repeating that performance to-day, though they will find the Australians a difficult lot to shake off. They are very fast both forward and back, and now that their combination has improved they are little inferior to the famous MI Blacks. Unfortunately Messenger who did such wonderful goal-kicking when last here, will be an absentee through an injured leg. This improves Warrington's chances, but it will be no surprise if they are beaten. The Kangaroos' feat at rfunslet last Saturday was one no team in the Union could have achieved.

Barring Messenger the visitors will be at full strength—including the young kangaroo, who travels as the mascot of the tourists. The selected side is: Full-back : stones. lbs. Bolewski Three-quarters : Bailey Devereaux Morton l2 3 Frawley

Half-backs : . Holloway Deana Forwards : Burdon l3 0 Davies Abercrombie O'Malley • 11 5 Graves Walsh

The Warrington team will be : Full back : Stone. lb. _ H. J. Tilley ll 8

_ Three-quarters: J. Fish G. Dickenson I. Taylor ll 10 L. Troharne lO 8 Half backs : E. Brookes J. Jenkins lO 10 Forwards: A. Boardman P. Boardman G. Thomas W. O'Neil l3 7 W. H. Dowell l3 4 J. W. Cheaters

This is about the best side the Club can command, and if anything like last week's form is maintained, should give a good account of itself. Much satisfac- tion will be felt that Dickenson is fit, and the appear- ance of Treharne on the wing should also arouse interest.

The following is the Kangaroos' war cry : Wallee Ifullara Choomooroo ting-al, nth ! Nab Nab ! Nab ! Canai, Barrang, Warrang, Warrang, Yallah, Yallah, Yallah, Yallah, Ah ; Jaleeba booga boorooloong, Yarnah meei, meei, meei, Neeyarra, Weyarra, Jaleeba, Cahwoon, Cooeewah, Cooeewall, warr, wooh TII.NSLITION : We are a race of fighters, decended from the war gods, Beware ! Beware I Beware ! Beware! Where we fight there will be bloodshed; Go! Go! Go! Go! We are powerful but, merciful, are you friends ? good, good, The Kangaroo is dangerous when at bay ; Come on, Come on, to death. --- Following the match the teams will b., entertained to tea by the Warrington Club at the Lion Hotel, at 5.30, afterwards attending the first " house " at the Hippodrome.

Warrington have an excellent chance of reaching' the semi-finals for the Lancashire cup. Their victory in the first round at St. Helens on Saturday was ;pined in brilliant style, and the team showed signs of having come into form at last. They have not had the luck. to be drawn at home even in the second round, but they should be equal to beating Swinton on any ground, even though the " Lions " did defeat the Wirepullers by 5 points to 3 early in the season. Warrington are always about twenty-five per cent, better in a cup-tie and I do not recollect them ever being beaten by Swinton in a knock-out contest.

The draw for the second round of the Lancashire Cup is as follows : Swinton v. Warrington Oldham v. Rochdale Hornets Leigh v. Wigan Barrow v. Runcorn. To be played on November 21st.

There must have been some unbelieving faces at Wilderspool when the half-time message arrived. It is certainly not usual for Warrington to score 18 points in the first half of a match away from home, and this season in particular such feats have seemed quite beyond them. On form it looked likely that St. Helens would secure the verdiet,though I ventured to predict last Saturday that if Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Brooks displayed their old form, Warring- ton would win. This is just what happened. The absentees all found their feet at once with the result that the couple of hundred supporters from Warring- ton were treated to a display which was like balm of Gilead to their despondent souls. After all,Warrington had not forgotten the finer arts of football. The way they scored 2 goals 5 tries before the interval quite flabbergasted the St. Helens folk, who had anticipated an afternoon's entertainment of another kind. St. Helens were quite out-classed and could rarely struggle to half-way, while the Warrington combination fare and aft was brilliant.

It cannot be contended with justice that Dickenson, Taylor, and Fish are "done." They appeared to be going backwards in the early part of the season, but training has worked wonders. Fish is still the best left wing in the Union when the mood takes him and his feet are not clogged by inattention to training. If he would only get about 141bs of that superfluous fat off, he would Boon be dazzling the eye of the crowd as of yore. It was remarkable how well he ran on Saturday, and to score three tries was no mean feat. As a matter of fact he crossed the St. Helens lino on five occasions, twice being adjudged off-side, and the way he made rings round Th.nchester didn't half suit the local enthusiasts. If Jack will just make the necessary effort he will earn further honours and perhaps get the benefit which he had almost forfeited his claim to. Even the New Zealand trip is not out of his reach. All he has to do is cast a smaller shadow when the sun shines, and score a try or two each week.

Dickenson played fine football at centre and in addition to serving Fish well he scored the try of the day in masterly fashion under the posts. It was a great pity that his weak knee caused him to retire five minutes after the interval. The captaincy has brought the lengthy George little luck this seasin. Taylor also seemed more like himself, though not as fast as he should be. On the defence he and Tilley were great. Brooks made a wonderful difference behind the pack even as scrum worker, and Jenkins gave one of his cleverest displays. A word of praise is due to the forwards who quite outplayed the St. Helens six. The young ones, P. Boardman, Cheaters and Hewitt, were the best of the bunch. St. Helens played a very ragged game, but they have a young player on the left, Flannagan, who shaped like a coming star. He has speed, pluck and trickiness. Bradshaw found him rather a handful.

Brooks should know something about the "Kangaroos "by the end of the month. In addition to to-day's match, he will play against them at Everton on Wednesday the 18th, for the League team, and on Wednesday the 25th at Wigan for Lancashire. The full teams are given in another column. ' The case of attempting to bribe Todd and Johnston to " lose " the Wigan— flunslet match has ended disastrously for the tempter. Edward Croston, a Wigan coal-dealer, was on Wednesday sent to gaol for two months' hard labour under the Bribery and Corruption Act, and in my opinion he deserved al'. he

Hockey.

Latchford defeated Widnes last week by 3-1. The game was of a fast and strenuous nature and there was little to choose between the two sides. The score at the interval was 1-1, and afterwards Banks obtained the lead for the home team after some clever passing and later on Parker scored the third goal from a good follow up.

The second teams met at Widnes, and Latchford won 2-1 after a grim struggle. The result was quite satis- factory as the visitors had not a very strong team and the ground was all agiinst fast hockey. Booth scored both goals for Latchford.

To-day the First Eleven meet Sale Moor at Sale with the following team :—W. Unsworth, goal ; Wilkinson, T. Dixon, backs ; D. Travis,J. B. Wilkinson, J. Albinson, halves ; F. Newell, H. Parker, H. Scott, E. Banks, W. Brown, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. B. Cobb. Train leaves Arpley Station 1.14 p.m.

The Second Eleven play Mr. Wilkinson's eleven with the following :—J. Appleton, goal ; S. R. Rumviey, L. Eenworthy, backs ; A. Newton, A. Knight, F. Tole, halves; F. Dunn, R. Jones, H. Booth, W. EL Minards, C. Hackney, forwards. Bully-off 3 p.m.

THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

On Thursday evening, the Warrington Musical Society gave its first concert of the season. The programme included Barnett's "Ancient Mariner " and a miscellaneous second part. Coleridge is perhaps the most =sleet of English poets, and there is a harmony and a swing about his " Ancient Mariner " which make it peculiarly adaptable to Datleic. Take for instance the lines : "And now 'twas like all instruments Now like a lonely flute ; And now it is an angel's song, That makes the Heavens be mute." The words alone conjure up the spirit of music in a way that has never been surpassed in poetry, and with the accompaniment of Barnett's musical interpreta- tion, the effect is irresistible. Throughout the poem we continually com...e across these wonderful word pictures with their masterly musical rendering. Mr. William Waite (bass) took the character of the Ancient Mariner, and Mr. john Bardsley (tenor) per- sonated the Wedding Guest. Other parts of the poem wore sung as solos too. Miss Lillie Wormald gave a very admirable rendering of those lines : "The fair breeze blew, the white foam flew, The furrow followed free : We were the first that ever burst Into that silent sea" and Madame Amy Dewhurst,who possesses a contralto voice of unusual depth and fulness, sang most taste- fully in those lines beginning: "Oh sleep! it is a gentle thing." The bridal chorus was sung with effect by female voices only. In this and one other female chorus the ladies of the Society distinguished themselves. There was perhaps only one weak point in the chorus, and this showed itself in the finale, in which the tenors seemed not quite up to the mark. The second part of the programme, which was of a miscellaneous character, included an overture by Mozart which was admirably rendered by the Society's Orchestra. Miss Lillie Wormald followed with " Ardon gl' incense" from the mad scene in .` Lucia di Lammermoor," with flute obbigato by Mr. Lingard. This song, with its recurring trills is a very difficult one to ' render, and Miss Lillie Wormald's vocal powers were admirably displayed. The item MIS much l'appreciated by the audience. Mr. John Bardsley ' came next with a Flower Song by Bizet. His render- ing was most sympathetic and dramatic, and he responded to an encore with another love song. Madame Dewhurst then sang "Ah ! figliol " by Meyerbeer and met with much applause. She sang another Italian song as an encore. Mr. William Waite relieved the sentimentality of the pro- gramme by an up-to-date military song, "Le Tambour , Major," demonstrating the triumphs of a Drum Major with the fair sex. He too was encored and sang a droll song in which he showed himself a master of humorous facial contortions. The concert ended up with a Polonaise of Glinkrt's for chorus and orchestra. It was sung with much sympathy and enthusiasm. ! Warrington is indeed to be congratulated on the whole performance. One seldom comes across such a very successful and largely amateur orchestra as that which Mr. Crossley has conducted for so many ' years, and its rendering of the" Ancient Mariner ' was a distinctive achievement as far as the instru- mental support to the chorus was concerned. One felt, however, that in the solo parts it was a little too loud. Altogether the concert was much appreciated by the audience, which was fairly large, and would in all probability have been larger but for the fact that there were other public engagements in the town the same evening. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

There is quite a miniature musical comedy on the Hippodrome stage this woek,when Ur. H trry Roxbury presents "The Cowboy Millionaire " with the famous comedian Tubby Edlia ia the title role. It is a rollicking, go-ahead extravaganza with Edlin on the stage nearly the whole time. He has the audience in roars of laughter, and is well-backed up by Mr. Roxbury as Major Titus Blazes and a strong company. The other turns are generally of a high class character. Moray Cash, the popular comedian, gets a great reception with his character songs, chief among which is the old favourite " Bed bed, beautiful bed." Elmo and Rego provide some original acrobatic funniosities in a turn of real merit. They are ttvo of the smartest tumblers and knock- abouts we have seen for some time. A unique enter- tainment is given by Burnetti. He not only plays excellently on many instruments, but impersonates artistes like Van Biene, Chirgwin and others. Even as a quick change artiste he would excel. Maggie Bowman and Tom J. Ilorton present a smart comedy duologue entitled " She loved a policeman." Both possess originality and keep the audience smiling happily while the duologue proceeds. Ilaston, the Yorkshire mimic, also meets with success with his droll narratives and clever farmyard imitations. Stidder arid Dunbar appear in a musical comedy turn, introducing some clever 'cello variations. Mr. Widdop's orchestra delights the house with excellently played selections from grand opera, and the biograph pictures illustrate life in the remote Isle of Skye. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week the great attraction will be Herbert Clifton who recently, after severe tests, had his voice insured for £l,OOO. He will provide a most inter- esting turn which cannot fail to please. Another novel feature will be Clown Barker and his relined comedy animal act, assisted by Miss Rozsika. The entertain- ment will also be taken part in by :—Minnie Kirby, comedienne; W. H. Downs, Australian ballad vocalist; Willie and Johnnie, coloured acrobats and hand balancers ; Pop Dearly, the new style comedian and dancer ; Dave O'Toole, comedian (Edith Chantler, juvenile soprano.

Local Curacies. The Bishop of Liverpool has licensed the following clergy to curacies : —Rev. J. W. Glendenning, M.A., to St. Barnabas', Warrington ; Rev. T. W. Griffiths, 8.A., to St. Thomas', Ashton-in-Makerfiehl ; Rev. G. Walmsley to St. Paul's, Widnes. " Y " Branch N B.W.T.A. On Wednesday evening in the Silver-street Girls Club a White Ribbon Band was formed which will be affiliated to the Warrington "Y ' Branch. A White Ribbon Band is a band of girls in a factory, laundry or club who have signed the pledge and are united for the purpose of stimulating their own interest i and that of others n the Temperance Cause. These bands have been formed in many large towns and it is hoped that our Warrington band will prove as suceessful as others have been. Miss Florence Beswick was elected President of Silver-street White Ribbon Band, and after her address explaining why this band was formed, and its work, forty-seven pledges were taken by her and several "Y "members. During the evening hymns were sung and a solo 'Hiding in Thee" was sung by Annie Creaghan. SALE Now ON. UNDER THE CLOCK. Surplus Stock of Boots and Shoes.—Advt. Warrington Girl Found Drowned. Mr. F. A.. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Police Station on Tuesday, concerning the death of Gertrude Litherland, 15 years of agc., sten-daughter of William Bridge, 39, Hopwood-street, who mysteri- ously disappeared from home on Friday, October 30th. —The evidence was to the effect that deceased left home at 8.20 on the morning of October 30th, pre- sumably to do some cleaning at a house where she had been employed. She did not return, and informa- tion was given to the police. It was found that the 'girl had been discharged from her situation in Brick- ' street. She tried to pawn a brass ring at Messrs. Jackson's pawnshop in Church-street, ,but they would not accept it without an explanation from her mother, and nothing more was seen of the girl until her body was recovered from the Mersey on Tuesday morning by William Henry Sloane, 23, Back Crossley-street, who was at work `on a dredger.—Detective Woolla- cott said deceased's clothing had been torn by the passing boats. Nothing was found in the pockets, and there were no marks of violence on the body.—The jury returned a verdict of " Found drowned= Institute of Journalists. A quarterly meeting of the Liverpool District of the Institute of Journalists was held in the Liverpool Press Club, on Wednesday evening, Ur. T. Weston (chairman of the Warrington Sub-District) presiding. As one of the district delegates, the Chairman gave a review of the proceedings at the Manchester Confer- ence, and reported as to the result of the recommenda- tions made by the district on the questions discussed there. It was unanimously resolved to support the Inomination of Mr. Arthur Spurgeon, F.J.1., of Plymouth, as president of the Institute for the present year in succession to Mr. Woolacott, resigned. Mr. A. H. Ward-Jones was reappointed group representa- tive on the council, and was also elected to represent the Liverpool District with the North and East Lancashire District on the Orphan Fund Committee. The meeting considered a suggestion from the central office that the district should contribute towards a fund to meet the expense of taking actuarial advice for the preparation of a scheme of sick and unemploy- ment benefits, and the hon. secretary was instructed to reply to the effect that as the Liverpool District had favoured a compulsory saheme, and as the con- ference had decided on a voluntary scheme, the district did not feel inclined to make any such con- tribution.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES..

WIDNES MAYORALTY.

lAt a special meeting of the Widnes Towa Council on Monday, Councillor Tom Smith was unanimously elected mayor of Widnes, upon the proposition of I Alderman W. W. Gossage, seconded by Alderman J. W. Waring. The selection of a councillor to fill this dignified position is the first time in eke history of the borough. Besides holding the position of chief magis- trate, he is chairman of the Prescot Board of Guardians. WIDNES ROAD TROUBLE. For over a week Widnes people have been greatly inconvenienced by the closing of Ditton-road, the main thoroughfare to Liverpool, in consequence of a dispute between the Hutchinson trustees and the Widnes Cur poration. The trustees claim to own the road. On INfonday the road was opened again without prejudice to any litigation, which might arise, or to the cost of making it.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
FATAL FALL FROM A W kREHOUSE.

lAt Runcorn Docks on Monday morning, George Langley, of Ashridge-street, Runcorn, warehouseman, fell from one of the bays of the china clay warehouse on to the quayside. lie alighted on his head, and expired almost immediately. ; The inquest was held at Runcorn on Wednesday evening. A youth named Arthur Kearsley, a crane- driver, said Langley was standing on the platform of the warehouse and leaned over to put the catch of the tub on, when ho fell head first from the edge of the platform. Witness said Langley did not give any signal to lower the tub, and he simply swung the crane when he thought Langley had finished.—The Coroner said the method of lowering the tub should not be left to the option of the cranedriver, and added that there was something lax about the signalling. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and added a rider that the warehouse platform should be further protected.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
SAD DEATH OF AN OLD RUNCORNIAN.

The body of Mr. John Stringer, one of Runcorn's oldest newsagents, was recovered fron the Bridgewater Canal on Monday. It transpired that the deceased was on a visit to a friend's house, which is situate on the banks of the canal. Oa returning home via the canal- side the old man, whose sight is very bad, appears to have walked into the water. A splash was heard by a boy, who informed the occupants of the Foresters' Arms. Further assistance was procured, and after an hones diligent search the body was recovered. The police were soon on the scene, and conveyed the body home on the ambulance. The deceased gentleman when recovered, had his walking-stick gripped firmly in his hand and his spectacles on. The inquest was held at Runcorn on Wednesday night. It was stated that deceased was very near sighted, and in order to avoid bumping into people on the streets he preferred to walk on the canal bank.— William Byfleld, captain of the Grappling Corps, said at the time the body was being taken from the water by the corps' appliances Mr. Stringer's son was singing at a concert in aid of the corps' funds.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A meeting of the Runcorn Board of Gurrdians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. T. Baxter presided and there were also present :—Messrs. J. I Spruce, C. Roylance, J. Bolshaw, G. Egerton Warburton, J. Stokes, P. Booth, C. E. Linaker, J. H. Davies, T. Wright, H. Mawdsloy. S. Woodward, C. Horton, G. Watson, R. Nixon, S. Taylor, W. M. Morris, P. Collins, A. R. Norman, J. THE POOR I.A.W CONFERENCE.

The report of the delegates who attended the North- Western Poor Law Conference at Ulverston, Messrs. Woodward, Pilling, Chadwick and Wright (the latter at his own expense) was presented. The President in his opening address referred to the present depression in trade and pointed out that the " Labour Gazette" for September stated that out of 208 trade unions 89 per cent. were reported as unemployed at the end of August as compared with 4 per cent at the end of August 1907. The number of paupers in the country on the Ist January was 7833 more than on the Ist January, 1907. He also referred to the great increase in the lunacy and casual classes. Alluding to I the Old-Age Pensions Act, he said he would like to see I some scheme of insurance as in Germany initiated by the Government by which workmen would provide in early life against unemployment or unfitness for work. The report reviewed the various papers presented to the conference and recorded that the conference bad appointed a sub-committee consisting of two represen- tatives from each union to inquire into and report as to the best means by combination of unions or other- wise,of providing for the treatment of phthisis patients. Mr. NVoodward presented a detailed review of the proceedings, lie said they would all be exceedingly sorry that Sir John Hiobert, who had so long and 11.'4 presided at these conferences, passed away on Saturday. It wee a great loss to the community, for Sir John Hi hbert constituted in himself in his reports upon the administration of the Poor Law, the central attraction of the conference, and it would undoubtedly be a most difficult matter to find an efficient successor.

Oa the proposition of Mr. Morris, seconded by Mr. Hallows, the reports were adopted.

HIGHWAY MATTERS.

The Clerk repoiiec-ITIM-t--a.t-ri in-eetitii of the Highway Executive Committee he stated that the best terms he could arrange with the owner of land in Overton opposite Overton Hall which the Council required for widening pnrposes was 4s. per yard for the land and the erection of a suitable wall. The Surveyor reported that 105 yards of land would be required and that he estimated that the cost of the removal of the soil and the erection of a wall and fence would be .£63 7s. It was resolved that the owner be informed that the Council was prepared to widen the road and erect a wall and fence but they could not see their way to give anything for the land. The report was approved. THE ABUSE OF MOTOR TRAFFIC. _

The Clerk reported receipt of the following resolu- tion passed at a meeting of the Road Union : " That this conference of delegates from local authorities throughout the country earnestly calls upon the Govern- ment to introduce without delay fresh legislation for the better protection of the public from the abuse of motor traffic." The Council were invited to appoint a representative on the committee. It was desired that the committee should be as representative as possible. The Clerk said the Council had not hitherto been represented at the meetings in connection wiih the Union.

Mr. Linaker proposed that the Council should support the efforts of the Union. They had heard a great deal about tho abuse of the highways by motors of all descriptions, and if they could do anything to assist the Society to mitigate it in any degree, it would Le to everybody's satisfaction. Mr. Rovlance seconded. Mr. Hallows moved that the Council should send a delegate to the committee if it was thought that the organisation could do anything to stop the nuisance. In Stockton Heath which was thickly populated and where there were several dangerous corners, strangers went dashing through in their motor cars. It was time something was done in the matter. The Clerk pointed out that the expense of sending a delegate would be rather heavy as the committee would probably meet a number of times and the meetings would take place in London. Mr. Hallows amendment was not seconded. Mr. Linaker's proposition was agreed to. HILTON SEWERAGE SCHEME._

The Clerk reported that at a meeting of the Halton Parochial Committee tenders for the sewerage of the parish in accordance with the specification and quantities prepared by Mr. J. Diggle, C.E., of Heywood, were opened and considered. It was resolved that the Clerk prepare a statement showing the relative prices contained in the four lowest tenders for carrying out a scheme embracing the under-mentioned districts :—(1) Halton-road as far as the brook and Sea-lane, (2) Halton-road, Sea-lane and the village with the excep- tion of Spark-lane, (3) the whole scheme except Heath-road.

ST. HELENS.

-- FT. HELENS AD MISSIONARY WORK. The Rev. Cyril Bardsley, vicar of St. Helens, presided at the annual meeting of the St. Helens auxiliary of the Church Missionary Society, at the Town Hall, on Monday night. There was a very large attendance._ The Rev. I'. W. Ketchlee (treasurer), in submitting the financial statement, regretted very deeply the death of Sir Christopher Gamble, Bart., who had helped the auxiliary to a great extent. The contributions from St. Helens churches had been £613 14s. id., and £6Ol had been forwarded to London. Last year the total was greater—viz., £627 18s. 4d.—The Chairman spoke at length on the great need there was for increased activity In the mission fleld.—The Rev. J. E. Hewitt, of Bengal, and the Rev. Marrnaduke Washington. of the Central Committee, also spoke. TRAGEDIES OF THE MINE. , James Wolstenholme, Nowton-road, Parr, St. Helens met with a shocking death at the Havannah Colleries early on Monday morning. Wolstenhalme was a single man, employed by Messrs. R. Evans and Co. It is stated that deceased and Joseph Henry Mather were engaged during the night in timbering a road-way. About two o'clock in the morning Wolstenholme was engaged holding a piece of timber while Mather was sawing a piece off, when a huge fall of roof took place. Wolstenholme was buried and smashed to death, while Mather was seriously injured about the legs and body fie was removed to the Haydock Cottage Hospital. Wolstenholme was about fifty-six years of age, but he had no relatives in the district. eAnother fatality also occurred at Ashton's Green Colliery, where Patrick Kenny, aged 28, of 35, Tickle.. street, was killed by R suddea collapse iii a roadway. Kenny was a haulage hand, and was in company with a drawer named Edward Hilton, in the shunt at the bottom of one of the haulage brows. They were taking coal boxes along when a large stone suddenly gushed out of the side of the road and brought the place down on the two men. Kenny was buried, and it took nearly three hours to dig him out. Hilton was badly hurt, and he was taken to the St. Helens Hospital.

I MAYORALTY OF ST. HELENS. At the annual meeting of the St. Helens Town Council, on Monday, Alderman Bishop proposed that Councillor Alfred John Foote should be elected Mayor. I He said that Mr. Foote had been a member of the I Council since 1889. He was a sound business man, who enjoyed the confidence of his colleagues and towns- people generally. Councillor Collins, who seconded, said he had known Mr. Foote for considerably over 40 years. During the whole of his career be bad proved a sound, careful business man. Ile had worked with exceptional energy and devotion as a member of the Council. Councillor Foote was then elected unanimously. After returning thanks he outlined the work that was likely to be undertaken during the coming year. He said he trusted they would do something to find employment for the people who were out of work. He was glad to say that unemployment was not so bad in St. Helens as in many other places. He also dealt with the financial state of the borough, and pointed out that the total debt gave an average of a 17s. 2d. per head of population' as against the average over the county boroughs of England and Wales of £lB 9s. 2d. St. I Helens was thus £lO per head less than other county boroughs. If any manufacturer wanted a place to start work in, St. Helens was one of the best. The ratings were not high, there were cheap gas and electricity, railway and canal fecilitities, and abundance of labour,

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

MR. SEDDON, M.P.,. AND CONSUMPTIVE SHOP ASSISTANTS.

_ S_peaking in Gorton on Sunday, Mr. J. A. Seddon' spoke of the conditions under which shop assist- ants worked. He knew of cases where girls and women were working a hundred hours per week of seven dare, and who had no holidays from one year end to another. (Shame.) Owing to the long hours consumption was more rife amonm them than in any other class of workers. In Cheshire there was a sanatorium for con- sumptives, and over 50 per cent, of the young women who applied for admission were shop assistants. If all the shop assistants were united and they kept up their agitation they would be able to impress the Government with the ur-- of their need-

The amount advanced in mortgage was nearly £10,519, the largest for 30 years. RESCUE AND PREVENTIVE WOrtr IN soura WEST LANCASHIRE AND LIVERPOOL. ' During October the eight Police Court Missionaries and one Auxiliary Missionary employed by the Liverpool Diocesan Branch of the C.E.T.S., were kept very busily occupied. They dealt with 1465 persons in the Courts and Bridewells, visited 811 cases from the Courts and saw 415 others either at their homes or at the Society's offices. In order to try and help those who came under their notice they wrote 354 letters many of which brought about encouraging results. At the present time it is except:onallvdifficult to flnd work for those who have broken the law, but in spite of this the missionaries not only gave work tickets for the Fire- wood and Mat Factory at Bootle to 19 men, but also foun.l employment for 10 other men and lads, took 11 lads to Homes or put them to sea, sent 11 others back to their parents or friends and averted loss of e.nploy- ment in G instances. Women and g:rls also received their share of attention as 21 were sent either to their own homes or to other suitable homes (including the Society's Home in Upper Parliament- street) and situations were found for 6. Clothing was supplied to 47 persons, and 121 received food in addition to the breakfasts which were given to 413 men and 401 women at the Prison Gate lkfission li own. Owing to a roost welcome grant of £25 from the Lord Mayor's Unemployed Fund,and of food tickets from the Central Relief Socicty, the Missionaries were able to help some of the most deserving cases with which they met, much more liberally than it would have been possible for them to do otherwise.—Lastly, there were 44 addresses in the Mission Room and 51else— where it took 120 total abstinence pledges.

A fine of £ls and costs was inflicted by the magis- trates at the Daresbury Sessions on Tuesday, on Joseph Gaal, 17, Hanover-square, Itegent-street, London. SAV., ho was charged with driving a motor-car at a dangerous speed at Appleton on Friday, September 11th. He mas also summoned for having false identification marks. Defendant, aho was represented by Mr. If. Fox, St. Helens, pleaded not guiay. Mr. William Long presided over the Bench. The facts for the prosecution, as stated by Superin- tendent Ennion, were that at 6.30 p.m. on September 11th, defendant, accompanied by another gentleman, mas driving in the direction of Warrington from Nant- wieh. When passing through Whitley he was seen near Norcot Brook to be driving at a very fast speed, which v.aa continued and increased up to London Bridge, Stockton Heath. The speed varied from 20 to 50 miles an hour. Some witnesses, who were experienced motor drivers, would say defendant travelled passed the Apple- ton golf links at from forty to fifty miles an hour, and ex-Police-Sergeant Gosling would tell the Bench that be "jumped " London Bridge. Fourteen witnesses stated that the defendant drove a racing car at various speeds up to CO miles an hour. One said the car was like an airship,' and another likened the speed to that of a mail train. Bertram S. Newcombe, Stretton, said the car passed the golf links at a speed of altout sixty miles an hour, and on arriving home he telephoned to the police. He could only see the letters " Al "at the back of the car. Cruelty to a Donkey. William Broomfield, of Cartwright-street, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Monday with cruelty to a donkey on Thursday, October 29th. A police officer stated that on the night in question he saw the donkey which was attached to a cart con- taining green-groceries, standing in Priestley-street. When the cart had moved on, he saw a large quantity of blood on the ground. He followed the trail of blood for several yards, and afters,' ards caught it up with defendant in Naylor-street. He examined the donkey and found that the teat had been severely cut and blood was flowing freely. The officer asked defendant what be had done it with, and he replied that he must have done it with the whip and the whip was composed of a leather ho t lace, which defendant threw away. The officer saw that the animal was in great pain, and ordered defendant tc Lake it home.— The Mayor said that it was a very serious case. He had never seen a town in which cruelty to animals was prevalent. Uo would give defendant a chance this time and fine him ss. and costs, but the next time he would be dealt with severely.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. Wesley Guild Festival.

The Rev. W. B. Fitzgerald, Connexioral Guild Secretary, has a winning way of appealing to the young people who have the privilege of listening to him. Preaching on Sunday morning last ia connection with the Wesley Guild Festival to a good congregation in the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, he took for his text" And be not conformed to this world ; but be ye transformed by the renewing of your mind, that ye may prove what is good and accept- able, and the perfect will of God " (Romans xii., 2), and told them not to forget the cultivation of their minds, as this was a great essential to Christianity.— In the evening he based his sermon on the text " For we are labourers together with God ; ye are his , husbandry, ye are God's building " (Col% i., 3-9), and I enforced the need of building upon the right rock—that of Jesus Christ. The anthem " Abide with me " was well rendered by the choir, and 'Air. E. Roberts' solos, both morning and evening were sung in a creditable manner.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
UNITED RALLY.

There was a unique and highly interesting meeting in the Church on Monday evening. It took the form of a circuit guild rally and the large attendance was con- vincing testimony to the vitality of the various Guilds. Dr. Bennett presided and tke principal speakers were : The Rev. W. B. Fitzgerald and the Rev. Arthur E. Sharpley, president of the Liverpool District Guild Council. Arn,ongst others present were the Revs. E. Blanshard Reeling, T. Miller, T. Gladding, and F. Welwyn Kernick. The CHAIRMAN said that about twelve months ago it struck him that the Wesley Guild had become such a force in the circuit that it ought to be officially recog- nised in the quarterly meeting like other sections of Church life. He brought the matter forward and Mr. Frank A. Harris was appointed the Guild Secretary to speak for the Guilds of the circuit at the quarterly meeting. The next step was to get the Guilds united in their efforts, for it was believed that by working in harmony they would gain in enthusiasm, power and spirituality. The result was the inauguration of the Council to consist of the Guild secretaries, one repre- sentative for every fifty members of each guild, and the ministers of the circuit, with the superintendent as president. One outcome of the formation of the Council was to enlighten them They found that some Guilds were not working on strict Guild lines and without exception the Guilds which had denarted farthest from those lines were the least full of Curistian enthusiestn. It was decided that all should work on the same system. The Wesley Guild was not a juvenile essociation. It had bcen in existence some twelve or fourteen years. It was carefully thought out by some of the wisest heads in the land and it was doing grand work all over the country. It helped members to develop their speaking powers, and if there i was one thing of import- ance to every young man t was to learn to express himself properly. They suffered more from shyness than f*orn anything else in their churches. It was not always brilliancy which counted. He would rather Bsten to the " wish-wash " of a consecrated worker than to the studied orAtory of a nominal Christian who was doing no work in the Church. He wen proud to preside over the first Guild rally promoted by the Circuit Guild Council because in a sense he felt himself to be the father of the Council. (Applause).

The secretaries of the following guilds then preseated very encouraging reports on the work in hand : —Bold- street, Bewsey-road, Padgate, Penketh, Sankey Bridges, Latchford.

The Rev. A. E. S!IARPLEti delivered art address on "Opportunity " which was listened to with keen atten- tion. There were, he said, golden moments for resolution and for action which, once lost, never returned. The story of all invention and discovery showed again and again bow thousands had missed the opportunity and by and by one man had come along and seized it. The same applied to business and commercial circles. The speaker instanced the careers of Isaac Newton, James Watt and Nathan Rothschild as illustrating the value of grasping opportunity. It must be the prepared, the cultured,and the thoughtful mind which grasped oppor- tunities and made the best of them. Ile would say to the young people of to-day .If you are to he ready for the opportunity of success in life when it cames, you must he prepared for a good deal of self-denial and toil." The unemployed problem was with us all just now. How would it ever be solved except by dealing with the personal factor? It was, unfortunately, very often the unemployable roan who was unemployed. He did not want work. One of them came to a friend of his (the speaker's) not long ago, and on being told that he could begin work in the dockyards the following morning, he replied " Yes, unskilled labour. I know what that means—l3s. a week and hard labour. No, thank you. I can tramp sixty streets a day end it's a poor street that does not turn up it penny." (Laughter.) Many people by indiscriminate giving to beggars and tramps at the door were keeping those men going, and at the same time making the great difficulty of the unemployable person in England absolutely insoluble. We should never cure it till we got rid of indiscriminaie alms- giving. We should have to go back to the old days of apprenticeship when every boy wbo left school was taught a trade, and get rid of unskilled labour. Muscle was not worth much to-day in labour, because machinery could do it so much better. We wanted to train the skilled mind for better work so that we might solve these problems, bat it depended in the last resort on the personal factor. Continuing the speaker said the circumstances of young life to-day were favourable As they never had been before in the history of England and of the world. We heard a great deal about.equality, but there were certain kinds of equality we must not expect to find. We could not say that in any certain sense there was equality of people in this world. Human equality was a dream ; it was moon- shine. It did not ex:st and never would. Men were not physically equal and they were certainly not mentaly or spiritually equal. He did not know whether even in heaven we would find universal equality. He did not think we would. So far as we could tell now, there were angels and also archangels who were over the angeb,. There was, however, an ideal to which our world was gradually but surely working, and that was equality of opportunity. Every boy or girl haviag the ability should be able to climb from the lowest place to the very highest that education could give and so have the grand opportunity of success in life. If it was only to a large extent a dream in the world, equality of opportunity was a fact in the Church of Christ. In conclusion Mr. Sharpley said the Wesley Guild stood before them as a splendid piece of machinery for the service of God and his Church, but the machinery was dead and inert until there came the breath of the spirit of God. Ile urged his hearers to have as their ambition the cultivation of a beautiful character which was the only absolutely necessary thing in life.

The Rev. W. B. Fitzgerald devoted his address to the work of the guild in connection with citizenship. They had first of all to make their young people good Christians, then thorough-going Methodists and finally intelligent and helpful citizens. The idea of citizen- ship was comradeship and service. The men who were most honoured, whose lives seemed to have eterni.y in them, were the men who had given some service to the town, the city or the State. It was not the Pharaohs, the Cmsars, the Czars, and the Kaisers who were of the most importance. They were only the milestones marking the various stages of the great march of human- ity. It was such men as John Howard, to whom they had a memorial in Warrington, Faraday, Tyudal, Garrison and Wilberforce, and women like Florence Nightingale who counted because they had given their ve;-ar best for their fellows. Then there was Rowland Hill who by his work brought men close together and Richard Baxter who brought man and God into touch with one another. Their names were brought to his mind again by the fact that be saw statues to their memory in Kidderminster the other day. There were two sides of service which they ought to render—the service of man for his fellows—social service they would call it— and the service of man for God—spiritual service. It was the bosineas of every Wesley Guild to train its members for citzenship, to teach them what it meant; and to make them feel that they had to do a work not only for their church but for their town and country. Urging the importance of studying the lives of leading citzen., Mr. Fitzgernli said that at one end of the social scale they bed a min like the late Lord Shaftes- bury who devoted all his wealth, social position and influnce to the welfare of tho very poorest, and at the other end they had Thorucs Burt, the miners' repres- sentative, who had Laen spoken of by one who knew the composition of the Home of Commons ivell, as one of the finest gentlemen in that Howe. Every Wesley Guild ought to undertake some definite kind of social work. There was a wonderful amount of nobility in the hearts of their young Methodists and it was coming out in a thousand different ways. They must help to restore the thought of God to its right place in the life of the nation. It was a sad thing when a nation began to forget God. He was afraid we were getting very near it, and if the young people did not help to turn the tide it would be so. To-day the majority of our population were out- side all the churches. They must not only try to bring them back but to keep within the church those who were growing up in their Sunday schools and who wore enrolled in their Gnilds.Wo needed all the social seri :me _ . .

that Christian men and women were capable of giving,but it was not social servi,-e merely. We could not make people better merely by drainage and sanitation or by solving the housing problem. There was a great and crying need for thcse things, but we should never raisa the nation to its proper place in the world until, in addition to doing such work, we remembered that God must be supreme. that Ills life inns: enter into the life of the people. Then all the other things would become easy. The selfishness and the greed that had prevented 113 from getting social reforms in the past would be done away and we would make real progress. (Applause). On the proposition of the Rev. T. AI rr.r.Ert seconded by the Rev. F. WALWYN KERNICK, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the chairmen and speakers and also to the organist and choir for their services during the festival.

A postman %yaws passing the premises occupied by Messrs. Wright, Garnett and Wright and others in Sankey-street at eight o'clock on Sunday morning when he saw a volume of smoke. He gave informa- tion to the Fire Brigade, who were soon on the spot with the steam fire engine "Captain." The men entered the premises at the back, but for a time could do little owing to the dense smoke. It was ultimately found that a fire was raging in the room occupied by Mr. F. Cooke, dentist, and in part of the premises used by Messrs. Wright, Garnett and Wright. Con- siderable damage was done in each case. A number 'of plans belonging to the architects were much damaged and some totally destroyed. Fortunately the firemen got the fire under control before it could spread to the other part of the premises. The origin of the outbreak is not known. Wanted to go on the Bench During the Police Court proceeding on Monday a great commotion was heard in the corridor leading t 3 the magistrates' Bench. A police officer was despatched to investigate and it was found that an old man under under the influence of drink had found his way into the building, but for the timely meeting with a constable, would have found his way on to the Bench. However, he was taken into custody and brought up the next morning on the charge of being drunk and incapable. His name is Charles Wade, a stranger. He stated that that he had lost a basket and he thought the police would be able to give him some atsistance. He was on his way to Manchester and if they would only let him off he would leave the town at once. He was ordered to pay ss. and costs or go to goal for seven days. The Late Mr. John Maginn's Will. • The will of the late Mr. John Magian, J.P., of Raddon Court, Latchford, Warrington, furniture manufacturer and dealer, and for several years a member of the Town Council, has just been proved at £11,168. Mr. Maginn, who died on the 15th of last August, bequeathed his household furniture and effects upon trust, with the use of the same, to his wife for life or remarriage, and then to form part of the residue. The residue and all his real estate is left upon trust to pay the income to his wife for life, but if she remarries, then to be equally divided between her and all the children. On her decease the said residue of his estate is to be equally divided between all the children. Probate of his will, dated 11th August, 1908, has been granted to his widow, Mrs. Malialah Nfaginn, and. his brother, Sir. Patrick Magian, and Mr. Felix Ma&oinii, both of Warrington, the executors, by. whom the property is sworn at £11,168 Is. 3.1. of which £3,013 3s. 9d. is net personalty. Warrington Women Circle. Mrs. Ellis presided at the Circle on Sunday, and gave an acrostic on the word " Joy "---" Jesus owns you," and the reversal of the letters—" You obey Jesus." She was assisted by Mrs. Tynan and Miss Mitchell. A. most instructive paper on the Sisters of Bethany was read by Miss Healey, who said that earthly tasks most not obscure spiritual realities ; the making of character was of supremo importance. Many Christian lives failed because they lacked energetic and hearty service. Often life's greatest work is done in resting times. They also serve who only stand and wait. Effective service would always be produced by prayer. The high estimate Christ puts on women, calling them friends, accepting their ministries, had done more for the world than the bustling energy' of Martha. Mary and Martha blended into one would make the ideal Christian woman active service and quiet contemplation. The greatest rids of life oft6n led to rich experiences, which were never developed in life's sunshine. Miss Emily Barnes was the soloist and rendered " Come unto Me " and " Tell mother I'll be there." Table, Floor, and Hanging LAMPS. Latest designs now showing. See windows.—J. S. GARLAND, 51- 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Alleged Theft From Mrs. Crosfield. Before Mr. Plowden, at Marylebone (London) Police Court, on Friday last week, a Langharn Hotel waiter, named Johannes de Nys, aged eighteen years, was charged with stealing and receiving a diamond ear- drop, value 9,50, belonging to Mrs. Crosfield, Bryn Eithin, Colwyn Bay.—Mrs. Crosfield said she was at present staying at the Langliam Hotel. pn Sunday she lost the eardrop, and did not see it again until she was taken by the police to identify it at a pawn- broker's, in Fitzroy-square.—An aesistent at the shop said the prisoner called there on Tuesday with the eardrop, and agreed to accept an advance of .f.,1 upon it, saying it was given to him two years ago by his mother.—Mr. Plowden, having ascertained that that was all the evidence, said it was quite consistent with the prisoner having found the eardrop, and then pawned it. That was a very wrong thing to have done and a very good reason for the hotel authorities dis- missing or reprimanding him; but it was not larceny, and he would, therefore, be discharged. A Soldier's Lapses. Ernest Rogers. an ex-soldier, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday with stealing a silver watob and 2s. 101 d. in money, the property of Henry Cullen, Longford-street. It appears that defendant who had belonged to the East Lancashire Regiment, came to Warrington on March 2nd on a visit to his aunt in Longford-street. Ile left the house suddenly and the watch and money which were the property of the lodger, were missed. In the meantime prisoner had been arrested at Leyland and sentenced to 9 months imprisonment for stealing 10s. He was rearrested and brought to Warrington and charged with stealing the watch and money. He pleaded guilty. A letter was read, written by the accused, in which he expressed his regret. He had coinruitted the offence while under the influence of drink. He had a ,good character when he left the army. He had served all through the South African war and hoped the magistrates would deal leniently with him. Inspector Williamson stated that the watch and money had not yet been recovered. The watch had been pledged in Manchester and afterwards redeemed. Prisoner was sentenced to a months imprisonment. ,

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

A meeting of the above Council was held in the I.L.P. rooms, Friars'-green, on Tuesday evening. There was a fair attendance, Mr. D. L. Owen presiding. The Secretary (Mr. E. Plinston) was also present.

- . THE ELECTIONS. Referring to the recent municipal election, Mr. Plinston said he was very sorry to say that the members of the Council did not go into the fight as they ought to have done. He had helped Mr. James Ball as well as he could during the contest. There was not a single Labour member in the Council now, and in Mr. Ball they had lost one of the best men they had ever had.

NE W MEMBERS.

A letter was read from Mr. Sinclair asking if the Iron Moulders Society could be admitted to member- ship of the Council. It was unanimously decided to accede to the application.

PROPOSED TRADES TALL. A letter was read from the Sheet Nfetal Workers Society, stating that at their last quarterly meeting it was unanimously decided that efforts be made to erect a Trades Hall. _ _ .

Mr. PLINSTON suggested that the friendly societies as well as the trade union branches be approached with a view to see if they would join them in the scheme. It was proposed that the Executive Committee be instructed to consider the question, with a view to framing circulars to be sent to the various trade unions and friendly societies. Mr. IUOOTLFIELD, in seconding, said that if the Hall were built it would tend. to keep people out of the public-houses. He did not want to run the drink trafllc down, but be thought it would be better if people went to the hall to spend their evenings. The resolution was agreed to. FINANCIAL.

The balance sheet fo;-theieiji-nding .10th September, 1908, was read and proved to be satisfactory. The income amounted to £3O 19s, 51, and the expenditure to .C2l 2s. 4d., the balance in hand therefore being ,C 9 is. Id. This was audited and found correct by Messrs. G. Leach and Mr. W. Bullock. . .

Mr. BULLOCK spoke of the excellence of the balance sheet. He stated that he had been a member of the Council since 1884, and had never seen such a large balance in band. (Applause.) He hoped they would per-evere and get an even larger one next year. The question arose as to the disposal of the money. Arr. G. LEACH stated that considering the number of burglaries taking place lately, he thought it advisable to deposit the money in the hank. This was seconded by Mr. DELANEY. Mr. RICHARDSON suggested that the money be pat to further use and not banked. This suggestion WAS not approved, and finally a resolu- tion was passed that the matter begeft to the discretion of the Treasurer.

UNEMPLOYMENT.

Mr. HALEY stated that at the meeting of the Inquiry and Labour Sub.committee of the Distress Committee three schemes were put forward to lessen the number of unemployed in Warrington. He believed there were 1500 out of employment in Warrington at the present time. Of these, 370 names were registered at the ILabour Bureau, there being 128 females and 2.12 males on the lisc. He was informed that the :lanchester unemployed numbered 2,000. He noticed that in addition to the £30,000 granted in loans to the Man- chester Corporation to enable them to undertake work fos the unemployed, a free grant of £lO,OOO had been given from the fund administered by Mr. John Burns. lie thought it advisable to ask for a free grant for Warrington. If the schemes which had been suggested were curried out it would considerably lessen the number of unemployed in the town, After some discussion it was agreed to request the Town Council to proceed with works of utility, as sug- gested by the Distress Committee, to mitigate the unemployment prevailing.

SNATCHED FROM DEATH AT WIDNES.

As a motor-car was travelling down Albert-road, Widnes, on Monday morning, at about 15 miles an hour a child of five years old ran straight in front of the car. —People shrieked and covered their faces, but a gentleman sitting with the driver leaned forward, and with wonderful presence cf mind caught hold of the child's clothing and dragged it into the car uninjured. The brake gear of the car was broken by the driver's efforts to pull up.—An eye-witness of the occurrence states that it tv. really a marvellous rescue.

SYR U P.

MY DEAR CHILDREN, I am pleased that so many of you are taking up the idea of working for the poor during the coming winter. It is a very good work indeed, and you will at least feel you have given help to some, even if you do not obtain a prize. I expect you are beginning to look forward to el. Christmas holidays; it's not many weeks, is it, to that happy time? Well, I hope you are all doing your best, so that each of you may have a prize to carry home. Don't forget our "Children's Corner." I want a Jot of entries for the Competitions before the holidays come round. FLORENCE E. DARK. -4--- POOR BERRY.

A famous comedian arranged with his green- grocer, named Berry, to pay him quarterly. But the bill came in long before it wais due, and the comedian, in great wrath, called upon him and addressed him thus: " I say, here's a fine mul-berry. You cent in your bil-berry before it was dew-berry. Your father, the elder- berry, would not have been such a gooce-berry. You needn't look so black-berry, for I don't care a straw-berry, and- I shan't pay you until Chr;stmas-berry. CLARA DAY.

Dear Uncle Ned,—As I was reading the "Corner," I thought I would write you a few lines. I think a nice Competition would be to tell the life of a pet dog. Most boys and girls are fond of dogs, and so am I. Every one knows something about dogs. I must now close, with best love. F. RAYBOULD.

-4.- THE SMALLEST DOG IN THE WORLD.

Nearly two hundred different kinds of dogs! Think of it! And yet this ie not difficult to believe; for, we have water dogs, and watch dogs, and sheep doge, and fighting dogs, and pet dogs, and sledge dogs‘ and carriage dogs; big dogs and little dogs, long-legged and short- legged doge; dogs for killing rats, and dogs for killing wild boar; dogs for use, and dogs for ornament.

Sometimes the fash'on has been for big dogs; and then what giants were suddenly grown! Why, there have been doge as large as Shet- land ponies! Then slender dogs were in Co- mand, and, behold! dogs like shadows, with legs like pipe-steins, came into existence. As for the ugly dog fashion—well, perhaps you will not think so, particularly if you have an ugly dog, but, nevertheless, the pug dog answers this demand.

Then there Is the little dog—the toy dog, as it is called. The smallness to which a'dog, can be reduced is remarkable; and if the size of the very smallest dog had not been officially recorded, no one could be blamed for doubting the facts concerning the little fellow. "Tiny," a lack-and-tan terrier, has the honour of having been the smallest full-grown dog that ever lived. He belonged to Lieutenant- General Sir Archibald Maclaine. of England, and in honour of his extreme tininess, :s now carefully preserved under a glass case. Tiny was less than four inches long, and could comfortably curl up and take a -nap in a common glass tumbler. An ordinary finger. ring was large enough for his collar; and when he sat up, a baby's hand would almost have made a broad and safe resting-place for him. Of course, Tiny was of no account against a rat. Indeed, a hearty. self-respecting mouse would have stood its ground against the little fellow. But if Tiny had not strength, he did lave courage, and would bark as lustily as his little lungs would let him at the biggest rat that ever lived—when the rat was dead.

To tell-the whole truth, Tiny was remarkable and he was famous, but he was not very happy. He could have hid almost anything he wished to eat, but he had no appetite. He shivered most of the time, even though he was usually hidden in warm wraps. Of course, he caught cold easily, and then, oh, dear! how pitifully he did sneeze!

PUTTING THIS AND THAT TOGETHER.
