POSTAL AND SAVINLS BANK REGULATIONS

For farther particulars see " Post Office Guido," at any Post Office. POSTAGE STAMPS. Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are !Jr on sale at each post office. Price 2s.

INLAND CORRESPONDENCE. The term " Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence" is divided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Half penny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trgns- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size.

LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows : Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... ld. For every additional 2 0z5.... Id. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth. POST-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 51 inches in length by 31 inches in width, or be less than 3f inches in length by 2t inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and fl-inch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACKET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 20z. in weight. Printed matter, &c., not in the nature of a letter, may be tsausmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is id. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Office aro treated in the pcst like any other printed matter. PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For a parcel ... ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 PP exceeding 1 lb. IP 2 lb. 0 4 PP 0 lb IP 3 lb. 0 5 If PI 3 lb. 11 5 lb. 0 6 PI 5 lb. I 7 lb. 0 7 If 7 lb. If 8 lb. 0 8 IP 11 8 lb. „ 9 lb. 0 9 9 lb. 10 lb. 0 10 PI Pt „ 10 lb. 11 lb. 011 No parcel may exceed 11th. in weight. The size allcrwed for an inland parcel is:— Greatest length... ... 3 ft. G in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. Gin, in its longest dimensions as much as 21t. Gin, round its thickest part ; a short parcel may he thicker ' • thus, if it measure no more than 3ft. in, length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving. PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The posta.ge varies to different countries and must be prepaid. MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issues single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a id. can be contninod in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 43.; £2O, Gd. ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10d. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order i may be issued for places abroad varies n each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, Gd. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by 6d., from Gd. to £l. Is. The poundage fees are :—id. up to 2s. 6,1. ; Id. up to 155.; lid. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should bo retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums..

TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Postmaster-General possesses,almost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. 'fhe charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of 6d. which covers 12 words, and an additional id. tor each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The coat of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handed to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must consist of two words, is £1 IS. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL) Must be written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK.

These are established at all money order ofTiCes. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, and the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is Is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the amount of each deposit . . . . .

_ should be entered at the time of paying in. If. the deposit is £1 or over a special acknowledgment is sent from the bead London office. Interest is allowed at the rate of 21 per cent. Any amount up to and including £1 may be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can bee xpedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LIND CHARGES. The fees for the use of the Trunk lines are as follows:— For 25 miles or under 3d. " 50 " " 75 „ If „ 100 „ „ /- „ every additional 40 miles or fraction thereof... _ 6d,

Between the hours of 7 p.m. and 7 a.m., six minutes conversstlon is allowed for the same price as three minutes between 7 a.m. and 7 p.m., aud a rediiced charge is made for three minute calls.

MOTOR CAR. AND CYCLE REOLTLATIONS.

_ Each one Must be registered with the council of a county or county borough, the fee being Z 1 fored motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A register a nuniber is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other effences. The highest speed at which a car racy be driven on the public highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the Motor Car Act (1903), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dangerous to the public, having regard to all the circumstances of the Case. In the event of an accident to any person due to be presence of the motor car on the road, the river must supply his name and address, and the twner and.number of the car, under heavy penal- odie The driver must produceh is licence at the sequest of a police constable.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

NO NERVOUS DISEASES IN BABYLON.

_ Professor Frederick Delitsch, lecturer on Assyriology, has written an interesting paper on the Babylonians. He says they experi- enced all the ills our modern flesh is heir to except nervous diseases—these they did not know. Ancient Babylonians indulged in most of the food still eaten, and bread they called the staff of life. They drank a liquor made from dates, the equivalent of modern Kirsch- wasser, also wine, but the most popular drink was water. Life they characterised as: "Eat bread and drink water."

CONTAGIOUS DISEASES AND PURE AIR. Dr. Petereert asserts " that the human body is the reagent of the atmosphere, and reacts in health and diseme against every impurity in it." From this he draws the fol- lowing inferences: In tonfined air the germs of disease abound and poison the .system. It becomes pure only by freedom and exposure to the sun ; in infancy, light is a powerful stimulant to nutrition ; in all contagious die- eases and in eruptive maladies we obtain the beet results by exposure of the child to pure air as .long as possible ; we are able to ex- plain the good effects of this exposure by the greater purity of the air inhaled, and conse- quently the greater quantity of it, for Nature instinctively objects to inhaling that which is impure, and by the improved nutrition and the better digestion of food, for pure air, as well as light, promotes digestion and assimi- lation in a most remarkable manner.

HIGH TEMPERATURE AND RADIUM.

The question of the effect of high tempera- tures on radio-activity has been much dis- cussed, anclii different experimenters have arrived at different conclusions. In a com- munication made to a scientific gathering in Cologne, Messrs. Schmidt and Cermak claim to have discovered the reason of the discre- pancy. They find that with the radium in a new quartz tube, there is no change in the radiation on heating. When he experiment is conducted in an old tube, however, the diffusion of the products of combustion through the material of the tubes may cause an apparent increase. It is claimed that it has been shewn that temperatures up to I,soodeg. Cent, produce no change whatever in radio-activity. This tends to throw doubt on the view that radio-activity is due to the spontaneous breaking-up of the radium atom. Such high temperatures could scarcely fail to accelerate such a breaking - up.

===== 0002601_19081114_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
TREATMENT OF RHEUMATISM.

Dr. A. F. Schell-Schmidt states that he has had very indifferent results from the admini- stration of the salicylates, as a rule; his cases dragging along, week after week, without ap- preciable benefit from them. Lately he began a systematic exhibition of aspirin in acute rheumatism, and the results obtained have been exceptionally favourable. He gives the drug in doses ranging from ten to twenty grams, prescribing it in powders, and direct- ing the patient to suspend it in water by the aid of sugar. It is insoluble in water and also in acid media, passing unchanged into the intestines, where it is decomposed. As- pirin was found to have an action similar to salicylate of sodium without the distressing stomach disturbances of the latter. Its action in the author's hands was very rapid, very few cases requiring more than twenty-four fifteen-grain powders.

THE GREAT NEBULA OF ORION. One of the most striking spectacles revealed of late by telescopes, says Popular Scientific Siftings, is that of the great nebula in Orion. In the complexity of its glowing streams, spirals, and strangely - shaped masses, inter- cept4d by yawning black gaps and sprinkled over with stars arranged in suggestive groups and lines, it has few rivals in the heavens. The impression of astonishment matie by the sight of this nebula is heightened by know- ledge of its enormous size. The entire solar system would .appear as a tiny speck beside it. Yet this tremendous aggregation of nebu- lous clouds and starry swarms has now been proved by the researches of the astronomers of the Lick Observatory to be flying away from the earth and the sun at the rate of eleven miles in every second. But so vast is its distance that 100 years reveal no visual effects of the great nebula's swift retreat. If it were near by, it would seem to become rapidly smaller.

FUTURE OF THE CONTINENTS. Mons .Charles Lallemancl, the general director of Levelling operations in France, t.srges the desirability of an international understanding for the study of the evolution and future changes of the surface of cur globe. He recommends that in every coun- try, two or three times in a century, funda- mental measurements of level should be made. These should be applied even to re- gions apparently perfectly stable. Thus future generations, he says, would come to pcssess strict scientific data for determining the ele- vations or lowerings of mountain chains, basins and coasts, and the probable drying up or deepening of ports—in a word, would be able to predict the future of the continents."

WHY IRON RUSTS.

Dr. Cushman announces as a result of ex- tensive investigations that the rusting of iron is really due to electricity. He started \ his re- searches with the object of discoverizt somd method of preventing the corroSion of wire fences—a subject of vital interest to farmers. He found that the first attack on the iron is made by hydrogen in the form of exceedingly small particles, not as the text-books have taught us, by oxygen. This is in agreement with the modern theory that many sub- stances when they are dissolved in water are dissociated into ions, or minute particles smaller even than atoms, all carrying elec- trical charges. Even pure water contains a certain number of these. . Acid impurities multiply the hyd:.ogen ions and strengthen them in their corrosive effect upon iron. The, action is purely electric, and involves an ex- change of the electric relations between the hydrogen and the iron. Dr. Cushman dis- covered that active oxilising agents, such as chromate and biehromate of potash, prevent rusting by safeguarding it against attack by the hydrogen ion. By immersing the iron in a concentrated solvition of bicliromate acid, and then washing and wiping it, the metal is rendered passive, so that it becomes capable of resisting electro-chemical attack.

DESTROYING INSECTS BY ELECTRICITY.

Satisfactory results have been obtained at Zittau, Saxony, by an ingenious method of insect destruction. The caterpillars of a cer- lain kind of moth have been working great damage during the lest few years in the fir wOods of Germany, where many thousands of acres have become a prey to the voracious in- sect. These moths fly during the early hours of the night, from ten o'clock in the evening to one o'clock in the morning, and they are easily attracted by light. In fact, the night gleam of cities and lighted railway trains has been repeatedly found to determine the tracks followed by them. In the method em- ployed for destroying them powerful search- lights were erected, which throw their light ion the parts most frequently visited by n- sects from the neighbouring forests. The in- sects were destroyed by a fan exhauster placed close to the searchlights. A piece of wire gauze of about lcm. mesh was stretched out in front of the discharging opening of the exhauster, against which the moths are thrown. The apparatus used in connection with the definite tests comprised two search- lights; resistances for controlling the current; and flame-are lamps for further lighting the suction opening of the fan. Three walls. of the collector - box consist of iron 'wire gauze of 7•smm. mesh. After being filled until pre- venting the passage of an adequate air cur- rent, the collector is removed front the, fan and emptied of its content. A point of special importance, adcorcling to the Times, was to ase4rtain suitable elevated points for installing the electrical apparatus whence the most infested forests could be lighted as well as nossible. Those selected comprised the roof of the city electricity works, the tower of the city hail, the roof -of a municipal school, and that of a factory. Excellent rekults were obtained, _

Oldham 4 420 Salford Warrington's Fine Win at St. Helens. The first round for the Lancashire Cup saw the War- rington men in something like their old form, and they recorded a handsome win at St. Helens on Saturday before 3,000 spectators. Both teams turned out as selected in beautiful weather and a very fast game was witnessed, the visitors excelling at all points. Teams: St. Helens—Barton, back ; Manchester, Helsby, Hillen, and Flanagan, three-quarter backs : Trenwith and Hill, half-backs : Brier' Prescott, Smith, Pope, Mavity, and Mooney, forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, ' Taylor, and Bradshaw, three-quarter backs ; Brooks and Jenkins, half backs; A. Boardman, P. Boardman, Belton, Naylor, Hewitt, and Chester, forwards. Referee, Mr. H. Farrar, Keighley. Touch judges, Messrs. A. Jones, Runcorn, and H. Lee, Leigh. Warrington opened in determined style, the forwards rushing the ball to the home line, when Barton made a plucky save. The Saints tried hard to get away, but were overvratched and from a penalty, Fish opened the , scoring with a fine goal from near touch. Prescott ' broke away, and Tilley kicked the bailout of the hands. Dickenson attempted to get through, but was offside. Barton marked, and St. Helens' backs started a passing movement, but Hillen missed the ball. Hill passed prettily to Flanagan, who got away, but was forced in touch. P. Boardman cleverly intercepted a pass from Hill, and Barton saved whoa things looked black for St. Helens. Jenkins was tackled on the home line, and the forwards relieved. Fish raced over, from a cross kick' by Bradshaw, but had been off-side. St. Helens were now penned in their own quarter where Fish made a desperate effort, but was tackled, and Jenkins succeeded in getting over after Manclasster had mulled a low pars from Trenwith. Fish took the shot at goal, but failed. Fine passing and kicking gave Warrington a good position in the home quarter, but the home forwards made a strong dash to centre. War- rington forced them back, and Brooks passed to Dickenson, who dashed away, and at the right moment transferred to Fish, who again rushed over at the corner, but failed at goal. Hillen then dashed away and passed to lielsby, who returned the pass, but Hillen dropped the leather. and Brooks cleared. St. Helens showed better form, and made their appearance on the Warrington quarter-line. Brooks got away and passed to Jenkins, who transferred to IFish, and he succeeded in racing over the corner. Brooks this time took the shot at goal, and was successful. Trenwith broke away from a scrummage, and passed to Hillen, who kicked over the Warrington line. A good follow-up was made, but the ball rolled into touch. A fine effort was made by Jenkins, Taylor, and Bradshaw-, the latter losing the ball when tackled on the line. After some pretty pass- ing by Brooks, Jenkins, Taylor, and Dickenson, the latter got in under the posts, and Fish landed a goal. Warrington were again dangerous, but Briers relieved. St. Helens had a free kick close in, but the ball was kicked to the Warrington line, where Fish made a clever mark, which enabled Warrington to clear. Half- time :—Warrington, 3 goals 4 tries (16 points) ; St. Helens, nothing. A. Boardman restarted, and Manchester secured the leather, but was pushed in touch before be could get in his return. After five minutes' play Dickenson retired with an injured knee and Hewitt took his place. St. Helens made a big effort, and from a scrimmage near the Warrington line the leather was passed out to Hill, who tipped it neatly across to Manchester, who suc- ceeded in grounding the ball as Fish made a desperate attempt to tackle him. Play became most exciting,but the Warrington men by some strong play made their way to the centre, where some fine work was seen by Hillen and Flanagan, but they bad to meet with some smart tackling. Brooks passed to Hewitt, who handed to Fish, but he knocked on. A spirited rush by the borne forwards carried play to the visitors' line, and a try was only averted by one of the Warrington men knocking the ball back with his band. A free kick was granted to St. Helens, and Flanagan had distinctly hard times. A moment later, however, Flanagan fielded a cross-kick cleverly and beat the Warrington defeme at the corner. St. Helens lost the services of Mooney, and Warrington rallied, Fish being recalled after he had robbed Barton under the posts. He was next laid out with a knock on the head, but recovered and scored a brilliant try in the last ten minutes after being apparently surrounded. Final : Warrington, 3 goals 5 tries, 21 points ; St. Helens, 2 tries, 6 points. WIDNES v. WIGAN : THE VISITORS WIN IN THE SECOND HALF.

At Widnes before 4,000 spectators. The visitors had by far the best of the game, but half-time arrived with no score. Early in the second half Wigan showed their superiority. From a scrummage near the home line Wigan got possession, and passing between Gleave, Thomas and Todd ended in the latter getting over. No goal resulted. Wigan kept tip the pressure, and in less than five minutes.two more tries were added by Jenkins and Barton. Wigan were penalised in their own half, and Jones placed a goal for Widnes. The game became very vigourous for a time, and Lally, of Widnes, and Whittaker of Wigan, were sent off. Just before the finish Jenkins again crossed the line, and Thomas kicked a goal. Final :—Wigan 1 goal and" 4 tries (14 points), Widnes 1 goal (2 points).

Broughton Rangers visited Runcorn on Saturday and did well to make a draw of the match. There was no score in the first halt,- but on resuming Ilardyman scored and Barlow goaled. On Monday the game was re-played at Wheater's Field. On the Broughton side the only change was the substitution of Beetham for Winskill in the forwards, whilst Runcorn were compelled to make three changes, Deakin, Oakhill, and Kennedy taking the places of Flanagan, Watton, and Dickson. There were about 2,000 spectators present when the game started. In the first half Butterworth scored a try for Runcorn and Herdsman for the Rangers, both goal kicks failing. In the second half Hughes get a try for Runcorn and C. James for the Rangers, Jolly adding the goal points for the former and Barlow for the Rangers. QUESTIONS FOR TARIFF REFORMERS

to stop Imports in a trade' abolishes unemployment in that trade, how is it that the British shipbuilding industry, which is absolutely supreme in the world, and which has no imports at art, is one of the worst sufferers from unemployment P

-- The Board of Trade "Lour Gazette' for September, 1908, contains on page '270 the following paragraph : "Shipbuilding Trade Employment con- tinned bad. It was worse than a inotith ago, and much worse than a year ago. The,pertentage of Trade Union members unemployed at the end of August was 25.2 as'uompared with 2in July, 1908, and 03 in An,grig, 10117." _ Here, then, we have atrade in which: !.) .2 of the men employed —or, say, ene man in for—was out of -work from lack of work at the end of 'August, 1908. British shipyards are supreme. We turn out about two out of every three ships built by all the world. It is not because of ship imports, therefore, that there is unemployment in the ship- building trade. We are ship exporters all the time, and all vessels for our ownr register are built at home. Here we have a complete answer to the allegation that foreign competition is the cause of unemployment,and that only a tariff is needed to cure the evil. L. G. C. 11.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR. Municipal Contests and Religious Liberty. Sir.—lt is often stated that people at election times have a natural disinclination to probe into the meaning of words and phrases, alti are thus apt to place a mis- construction upon them, They have a fanciful desire for entertainment, and the mere attempt,on the part of a candidate for civic honours, to deal intelligently with what is essentially the ison d'etre of the friendly combat, viz. ; municipal affairs, results in either of two things—uproar or disaster. That statement may be exaggerated : I hope it is. But the Let remains that foreign matters.. am o intro- duced, and that they do not necessarily conduce to the selection of the proper person to fill the vacant posi- tion is sometimes seen. A new feature has been introduced into Municipal contests. It has been thought fit to deface certain buildings in a particular part of the town with placards bearing a strange device "Vote for So-and-So and Religious Liberty." The.phrase is complete in itself, I presume, for there is no further comment. But, sir, there are many who cannot understand what it means, and why it was introduced into the municipal election in, say, St. John's Ward. I will grant that " Religious Liberty " is not a new combination of words. It may be misunderstood, though. But if any two words ought not to be used with levity, and the use restrained by that due sense of propriety which such words demand—they are !` religion " and "liberty." They cannot have been coupled together and introduced into the election for no other purpose than that of hoodwinking the electorate. No political party would condescend to adopt such deplorable tactics, for apart from the Ignominy, the "burgesses would assuredly find them out, and sackcloth and ashes would not satisfy their shame. What, then, is the reason? There must be some religious motive, some import attached to, them, and their significance should not be stolen or lost. John Stuart Mill states that " there is always hope when people are forced to listen to both sides,' and I think, Sir, now that the elections are over, and folks have become calmer, the present, perhaps, is the most opportune time to venture to ask those who are respon- sible for the phrase to be good enough to give an explanation.—Thanking you for allowing me so much of your valuable space, truly yours, EDMUND OSBORNE.

Padgate, 11th November, 1901. -- A Call to Duty. Si;r-Will you allow men little space in your columns to draw public attention to a serious problem with whiela we are confronted? The question of nnemploy- ment.is engaging the minds of thousands of men and women in this country who are desperately in earnest that something must be done here and now for those who cannot find employment, and especially the women and children who are dependent upon them. The Government could do much, our local Council and various committees could do much, but while we are awaiting their final decision, men, women and children are starving in our midst. It is impossible to get and to keep a record of the number of unemployed as a large ma"ority of the workers are unorganised, and these in itaa main do not take the trouble to register their nainao. at the "Labour Bureau" because they are finding out that it cannot create employment but is simply a means of communication between ,employers and workmen, It is not on:y those who are out of employment entirely tiutt,are in need of help. There are large numbers now working short time; many of them are employed only two or three days a week, and the remuneratiomfor same is not sufficient to • purchase the bare necessaries of life where a wife and family are dependent. On_aeveral occasions of late I have visited the homes of some of our unemployed, and have seen and felt with them what poverty really means. The winter is upon us. There are hundreds of homes in Warrington at the present time with firegrates not heated, cupboards not full, children half-clad, and -women not dressed in tao best through no fault of their Own. Seeing that so rauchpavarty exists through unemploy- ment and that so many are too proud to beg, I would suggest that a start be made now to relieve the worst cases by someone offering -their services in each ward in the town who would inquka into all cases of poverty through unemployment that come to their notice and give what assistance they could by supplies received from anyone who would care to give for this purpose. I feel sure there are scorns who would willingly give something to relieve the distress during the winter if someone would undertake the trouble of investigation and distribution. f, therefore, am willing to make a start, and will gladly undertake to act in Whitecross Ward as a medium between all who care to give and those who are fortunate or unfortunate enough to receive. May I appeal, the; to all those who are willing to laclp to communicate with me, or if necessary I will mu:co a personal visit to all who desire it so that arrangements con be made. Cast-off clothing could either be sent to my address, or I could forward names and addresses of those who are most in ,need of the particular garments that anyone may care to give. With regard to shopkeepers and tradesmen who would care to help in the way of provisions, if they would let me know how much they were prepared to give weekly during the winter months I conld give notes up to that amount to the various applicants. I have ahead.; got the promse of 2CO loaves and a few other things. No amount would be tco small. Wila,t next, please?— Yours trulf,

NV. POOK,

THE ANNUAL MEETINGS.

The Examiner, Saturday, November 14, 1908.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

The Right to Work Bill.

MR. FRED J. REECE.

Farewell Supper and Presentation.

There was an interesting gathering at Toft's Assembly, Rooms, Bridge-street, on Tuesday evening when the staff of the Royal Court Theatre and several friends assembled to bid farewell to Mr. Fred J. Reece, for the past four years acting manager at the theatre, who is leaving Warrington in consequence of the change of management. Tne proceedings took the form of a supper and social and proved highly successful. Mr. G. D. Bradbury (manager of the Palace and Hippodrome), presided over a company of about fifty, including the whole of the Staff of the Royal Court Theatre, Mr. S. Smiley, (new assistant- manager), Mr. Tubby Ellin (who is now appearing at the Hippodrome) and Mr. Richards (Theatre Royal.) An excellent supper was served by Mr. William Warburton, Rylands-street. Opportunity was taken to present Mr. Reece with an illuminated address and purse from the staff of the Royal Court Theatre and friends. In making the presentation Mr. Richards spoke highly of Mr. Reece's abilities and hoped that wherever he might be, when he looked at the address he would always remember his staff and friends in Warrington. He had won respect from all with whom he come in contact and he wished him good luck and prosperity. (Applause). Mr. BRADBURY, Mr. Toot BOOTHROYD, Mr. H. L. EDWARDS (master carpenter) and Mr. A. NEWNS endorsed the remarks of Mr. Richards, and the company joined in singing" For he's a jolly good fellow." The address reads as follows:— To Fred J. Reece, Esq., Acting Manager Royal Court Theatre. In asking your acceptance of the accompanying present, we, the whole of the staff of the Theatre desire to express the deep regret with which we learned of yaur approaching departure from Warrington. We had hoped that the change of theatre proprietors would have made no difference to you, but we now understand that the theatre is to be worked in conjunction with the St. Helens Opera House, and not under separate manage- ment. Daring the four years in which most of us have experienced your unvarying courtesy and consideration we have learned to feel towards you something beyond ordinary respect, and it is therefore from that stand- point that we desire to place on record our hearty appreciation of your kindness towards us, and our very best wishes for your future good luck and prosperity. May every blessing attend you, is the earnest wish of each one of us. Signed by the Committee, A. NEWNS, H. WARUBRTON, T. BROWN, J. SINGLELON, H. CLARKE, H. L. EDWARDS. Mr. REECE, in reply, said that events such as that did not occur in the annals of every man's history but when they did, the remembrance of them must stand out like landmarks. He would not forget that evening and he thanked all present for honouring him as they had done. He wanted to take the opportunity of saying that during the period he had been in Warrington, now nearly four years, he had been most comfortable and happy. During that time he had enjoyed the confidence of his proprietors, Messrs. Johnson and Potter. He was sorry that pressure of business elsewhere prevented Mr. Johnson being present that evening. He sincerely regretted that dear old Mr. Potter was not still with them. As they all know he was laid to rest just recently. Mr. Reece, continuing, said he would like to inform the staff that if it suited their own arrange- ments they would all be retained by the new Proprietor and he was sure they would servo Mr. Beecham as diligently as they served the old manage- ment. He trusted that his other friends both present and absent would continue to remain good patrons to the Royal Court Theatre. (Applause.) An excellent musical programme was submitted by the following :—Mr. Tubby Eldin, patter and songs ; :qr. Joseph Singleton, songs ; Male Voice Quartette ; Mr. Dutton, songs ; Mr. Hannan, songs ; Mr. Tom Boothroyd, violin solo ; Mr. Richie, flute solo ; Mr. Laythain (of the Hippodrome) WAS the pianist. At the conclusion Mr. H. Wartecraros proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman ( Bradbury), and hoped that on any occasion when the staff of the Royal Court Theatre were having a social he would always be welcome because it showed the kindly feeling which existed between the staff of the two theatres. He also referred to Mr. Reece in most fitting terms. Mr. T. NEWNS seconded and also spoke of Mr. Bradbury's kindness in takieg the chair that evening. Mr. S. Wilson and Mr. William Warburton added a few words. A very enjoyable gathering was then brought to a close. Cheers were given for Mr. Reece and Mr. Braclb iry and all joieed heartily in siaging " Auld Lau; Syao."

===== 0002601_19081114_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
CROSS-COUNTRY RUNNING.

[By PEDo.]

Reports from all parts show that as the season goes on,club musters are increasing and enthusiasm growing. If this rate is maintained uatil championship time arrives, sensational doings will probably be recorded in connection with the various c intests,all the associations have already held at least one meeting and many have found it necessary to have more. All prominent people connected with associations in the North are working hard and particularity does this apply to the "Northern Cross Country" officials, who have busy times in store, as in addition to local and district championships, they ' will this season be responsible for the" National" and "International "races, which will probably be held within easy reach of Warrington. It therefore behoves local enthusiasts to keep their armour bright and all pull together,if they intend to render a good account of their doings. The Union of "Northern," "Midland" and "Southern" Associations, known as the" National," meets at Man- chester to-night. On a previous occasion when the race for the National Championship was held in the North, the Southern Counties seceded, and a repetition of this has rather raised the ire of the Northern and Midland I people, so that there will probably be some straight talking on at the Albion Hotel after 6 p.m. Suitable and amicable arrangements are expected from this mighty conclave, who will, however, get on good terms with themselves at the subsequent dinner. Liverpool and District had a special general meet- ing on Monday last when rules suggested by the Emergency Committee in connection with the Budge- Whitworth Cup were confirmed. The venue of champion- ships was also an important item on the agenda, as, owing to the decision of the Annual General Meeting to hold these earlier this season, there would be no chance of obtaining the racecourse :at Aintree. Further overtures are to be made, Parkgate, Bebington show ground, and the old racecourse at Newton-le-Willows being suggested. The latter would be a convenient venue for a great many clubs, and should at least prove more profitable, financially, than Hooton Park ; besides there is the qubstion of Ifolding the races over genuine country and with the majority of the clubs this is a moot point. A number of Salford Harriers were at Warrington Cemetery on Saturday last, to pay their last respects to Mr. Thomas Wainwright, an old colleague and club captain, who did a great deal towards the successes of that club some twenty years ago. SATURD RUNS.

SEFTON HARRIERS visited Liverpool Pembroke, a five mile run in the neighbourhood of Aintree, resulted in defeat for the District Champions, who were not fully represented, and their captain nearly got beaten for the individual honour. An attempt on the part of the Northern Cha-npion to win cleverly nearly resulted in disaster, and Clarke had to go all out to win by inches at the finish. Result :—Pembroke : Turton, 2 ; Millington, 4; Carrigan, 5 ; Howarth, 6 ; Alalcolmson, 8 ; toral 25. Sefton : W. T. Clarke, 1; McClaugliey, 3 ; Body, 7 ; Hocken, 9 ; Brady, 10 ; total 30 points. SEFTON HARRIERS decided their seven mile novice handicap. Result :

Min. Sec. 1. Clare (3min. start) 2. McLoughlin (2 nin. 30sec.) 43 5 3. Almond (2roin.) 4. Harrison (30sec.) 5. Whitfield (4min.) 6. Jackson (2min. 15sec.) 7. Hughes (2min. 30sec.) Burrows from scratch gained listest time prize, with 43 minutes. He was thereby barred from participation in the handicap prizes. If the distance was correct he is a very useful novice. CREWE HARRIERS followed a seven mile trail, and after an absence of nearly 50 minutes, H. Bennion returned at the head of the field. His nearest attendants were T. Edwards and T. Wilson, who are expected to make a good show in the Novice Championship next Saturday, when either may win if they are capable of beating Bradley. BROUGHTON HARRIERS had three packs running over a five mile course at Heaton Park. W. Scott and C. Tucker of the fast pack managed to split the slow and medium divisions. BURY A.C. mustered 32 .and ran over a seven-mile trail. A race for the novice section resulted in T. W. Hall annexing the prize. A good race is expected to-day btween J. W. Warburton and A. Wyatt, the , occasion being the fixture with Radcliffe Harriers, of whom Wyatt is a member, whereas Warburton, formerly of Radcliffe, now runs with Bury. WAniusoToN A.C. formed two packs to follow a trial laid by H. Holbrook and H. L. Clayton over about 61 miles of give and take country. The slow pack made good use of their time allowance of 71 minutes. ' J. George was first home followed by A, Siddall, W. Litherland and S. Hewitt; G. Watmough, A. Ford and W. Antrobus were leaders of the fast pack. To-day's fixture from the bridge Inn is a two pack run. The second division will be composed of this season's novices; all are seeking fame for the first time, and the results of the preliminary runs point to a close and interesting finish, which is timed to take place at the bottom of the new road, from Walton, about 4.30 p.m. by which time it is hoped Warrington F.C. will have recorded a victory against the Australians.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"The Breed of the Treshams."

Few of the dramas at present before the theatre- going public have attracted a greater measure of popularity than "The Breed of the Tresharns," and although this is the third occasion on which Mr. John Rutherford's famous play has been presented at the Royal Court Theatre, it has been followed with as keen attention and high appreciation as ever. Nor is this to be wondered at. The author takes us back to the stirring days of the Cavaliers and the Roundheads, when this country tNas distracted with civil war, and weaves a fascinating story of treason and intrigue, relieved with an example of heroism and self- sacrifice which never fails in its appeal to the emotions. Dominating the whole play is the complex character, of Lieutenant Reresby, who goes by the ill-sounding nick-name of " The Rat." Ile is a man with a shady past, whose sword and honour are for sale to the highest bidder. It is the child-like trustfulness of Margaret Hungerford, touching a tender chord in his memory, which arouses his better nature and nerves him to endure tortures to screen her cowardly brother and thus spare her the pain of seeing the man who in her eyes has stood as the embodiment of perfect loyalty, proved guilty of the meanest treachery to the Royalist cause. In the hands of Mr. Frank likrtram's principal company, the play does not suffer in the representation, in fact it could hardly be more realistically performed. Mr. Sydney Bland plays the exacting part of " The Rat " with consummate skill. His sang- froid when placed in tight corners is admirable and the dramatic intensity of his recital of the sore trials of his earlier years holds the audience spell- bound. One of the best features of the play is the way in which he holds the angry mutineers at bay by feigning drunkenness and then,to their discomfiture, turns upon them with loaded pistols. Miss Mildred Hodgson is a charming Margaret Hungerford and the naturalness and simplicity of her portrayal appeals to all. Miss Grace Harlington scores a great success as Batty, the inseparable companion of "The Rat." Mr. Alfred Holles is full of righteous indignation as Viscount Dorsington and Mr. W. R. Winning does well in the unpopular part of Colonel the Hon. Clement Hungerford. Mr. Howard Brenan is a dashing and impulsive Francis Tresham and Mr. George Traill distinguishes himself as the sympathetic Colonel Sir Henry Carwen. The minor parts are in capable hands and the play is splendidly mounted. As we intimated last week the theatre is now under new management, but Mr. Joseph Beecham, the new proprietor, has taken over the existing bookings. A successful future for this attractive and well-appointed place of amusement should be assured. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week Mr. Lingford Carson's No. 1 Company will present " The Great White Chief," by Lingford Carson, which is described as one of the greatest novelties in drama ever produced. A powerful cast will appear in their original roles, including Mr. Lingford Carson as Reindeer, the Dumb Chief, and Mr. Mansell Fane as Leo Stanley, the great White Chief. The scenes of the drama range from the prairie in South America to a Scottish banquet room.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

More About the Private Account.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. G. Mather presiding. There were also present : Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Coßingwood, Miss Broadbent, Dr. Murray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Billing° W. C. Brooks. R. Forshavv, R. Bolton, A. Brooks, J. Backhouse, W. Houghton, P. Longtou, W. Flanagan, M. W. Seton, E. L. Bent, R. Lee, A. Bottomley (clerk), It. W. Peacock (assistant clerk), J. F. Awberry (master) and A. Wright (superintendent of the Cottage Homes.)

===== 0002601_19081114_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE SIR .1!_1.• HI-EBERT.

The CILURNO.N moved: That this Board place on record its high appreciation of the long and valuable services to the county of Lancashire and the country generally, by the late Sir John T. Hibbert, Bart, K.C.B. who for many years was chairman of the North- Western Poor Law Conferences, and that the clerk be directed to convey to his relatives an expression of the Guardians' sympathy with them in their sad bereave- ment. _

Mr. HouGwrox seconded and said that Sir Jshn Hibbert open the Union Infirmary at Warrington, and had also visited Padgate schools and commented very highly indeed on both institutions. The loss of Sir John Hibbert was a national one,and would be felt from Whitehall down to the smallest Union in the Kingdom. Sir John could be called the prince of Poor Law administrators. Miss BROXDBInZT said the loss of Sir John Hibbert would be particularly felt at the present time when Poor Law administration was in a state of transition. Sir John Hibbert was like the Right Hon. John Burns, who always marched steadfastly forward, doing what he thought was right, whether it was popular or unpopular, without taking any notice of anybody. Mr. JOLLY supported and said that while Sir John Elibbert never lost sight of the great guiding principles underlying the Poor Law he was fully conversant with the minutest details. That fact enabled him in the best and highest sense of the word to be a progressive administrator of the Poor Law.lle was always awake to new ideas, while clinging very consistently to all that was good in the older parts of the system. Mr. FORSIIIW, as a County Councillor, referred to the esteem in which Sir John Hibbert was held by the members of the Lancashire Council.

Mr. BI.CEMOITSFI said he was brought up within a mile of Sir John Hibbert's residence, and Sir John was an ideal country gentleman. The resolution was carried, the members standing as a mark of respect. THE CH AIRMAN'S ILLNESS.

_ The CiiITRMIN made a sympathetic allusion to the serious illness of the Rev. T. R. Matthews, Chairman of the Board, and moved that the Clerk write express- ing the Board's sympathy with the rev. gentleman. Mr. BILLINGE seconded and said they hoped the great Head of the Church for whom Mr. Matthews had worked so long and nobly would sustain kim in these trying hours. The resolution was unanimously agreed to.

WARRINGTON LAW CLERKS! SOCIETY:

On Wednesday evening at the Winmarleigh Cafe, Sankey-street, a meeting of Wa.rrington law clerks was' held to consider the advisability of forming a Law Clerks' Society in Warrington on similar lines to those - now in existence and working successfully in Liverpool and other towns.

Mr. H. B. Houatrrott was in the chair and gave an able address in which he explained the objects and benefits of Law Clerks' Societies. The Chairman said that the Society would assist in giving an effective training in various parts of professional work, by classes,. lectures, discussions, or any other means which the Committee may from time to time deem expedient, would also endeavour to find openings for the employ- ment of members, and also to increase the friendly relations now existing between employers and employed in the legal profession and to promote social inter- course between the members. These were some of the objects of the Society. It was proposed by Mr. Witraam ALL iN " that this meeting representing the Law Clerks of Warrington are in favour of the formation of a Warrington Law Clerks." The proposition was seconded by Mr. Baines and supported by Mr. Antwis. Mr. SAM emus proposed that His Worship the Mayor of Warrington (Alderman Forshaw) be asked to accept the position of President of the Society for the ensuing twelve months. Mr. Collis remarked that it would be a graceful and fitting act on the part of the Law Clerks to ask the present Mayor who was a member cf the legal profession and well-known to all present to be the first President of the Society. The election of Alderman Forshaw was seconded by Mr. Baines and carried unanimously. The following officers were then elected: Chair ma Mr. R. Denham ; vice-chairman, Mr. Baines ; secretary and treasurer, Mr. Samuel L. Collis. A committee was also elected which consisted of the

lollowing gentlemen :—Messrs. h:^X's Sanders, W. E. Clare, R. W. Antwis, W. Sumner, J. W, Davies, H. Phsroh, H. B. Houghton and J. W. Bibby. After the election of officers and members of the committee the Chairman announced that meetings of the Society. would be held weeisly for legal debates and discussions, reading of papers on legal subjects and also that several meetings would be held during the- I session of a social character.

The SECRRITAItY proposed the usual vote of thanks, to the Chairman which was seconded and carried.

FAIR GROUND RIOT.

Warrington People Before the Wrexham Magistrates.

Guns in Use.

A riot, in which a number of Warrington people were concerned, took place on a fair ground at Coedpoeth, near Wrexham, on Saturday evening. It appears that the "fair " had finished business for the night, when a dispute arose between the fair people and some young men who were reinforced by villagers. Meanwhile, the showmen, anticipating a bad quarter of an hour had seized the Winchester Rifles at the suggestion, it is alleged, of their principal, and, point- ing their weapons towards the five-barred gate which separates the lane from the ground, fired. Several were wounded, and as soon as this got noised abroad hundreds of infuriated villagers began to pour down the lane, while the rattle of musketry was kept up without cessation. The casualties amounted to fifteen. It is alleged that while the five men who were afterwards taken into custody, fired,the sixth prisoner,a woman kept reloading the guns. While the battle was raging furiously someone had gone for the police, but when two constables arrived the shooting had stopped. POLICE PROCEEDINGS. Hundreds of people, mostly colliers from Coedpoeth, were present at the Wrexham County Police Court when, shortly before noon on 11,Ionday, six of the show people were placed in the dock on the charge of being concerned in the shooting of Hugh Jones and others, The names of the prisoners are James Cook, sen. ; James Cook, jun. (father and son) ; George Cook and his wife, Betsy Cook; and John Cook, all of Longford- street, Warrington • and Frank Hewitt, of Cefn Mawr. They were defended by Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, of Warrington. Police-constable Thomas Davies, of Coedpoeth, was the first witness. He stated that at half-past elevenon Saturday night, in consequence of what he had been told, he proceeded to the showground, which is situated in Jenny's Feld, at the back of the Prince of Wales Inn. Upon arriving there witness saw a large crowd of people. Upon going into the field he saw Betsy Cook, James Cook, and John Cook each holding a gun. Witness took possession of James Cook's gun, and Police-constable Watkins, who had also arrived, secured the one held by John Cook. Both these guns were loaded. The crowd rushed into the field, and the three prisoners referred to took refuge in one of the car avanS. The Clerk (Mr. LI. H. Jones) : Did any firing take ' place after they ran into the van ? Witness: The shooting was over then, sir. There was no shooting when we got there. Continuing, witness said that the crowd seemed to lose their heads altogether, and it was as much as he and the ether constable could do to keep them from breaking into the van in which the three had taken refuge, and also into a second vehicle in which the rest of the prisoners were. The crowd, however, smashed two tents and set fire to them, and some hurled them- selves at the door of each van, while others threw "Aunt Sally" balls at the woodwork of the van. Inspector Wyse : Did it appear as if the crowd were being kept at bay by these men with the guns ?—Yes. Cross-examined by Mr. Steel : And was the attitude of the crowd such as to make intervention on the part of the police useless?—Yes ; we could not prevent them. Should I be correct in saying that if these people had not been removed from the ground something serious might have happened ?—No doubt. It was very serious as it was. Do you think, from what you saw, that their lives were in danger ?—They were, sir. Dr. Stanislaus Furlong, house surgeon at the Infirmary, stated that three men were brought into the Infirmary suffering from bullet wounds. The first WAS Hugh Jones, and he had sustained a bullet wound on the right side of the abdomen, and another in the thigh. This man was operated on, but it was not considered expedi- ent to remove the bullet from the region of the abdomen. He was in a dangerous state. In reply to Mr. Steel, witness said the bullets were known as 22 shot. Inspector Wyse said it was not proposed tc offer further evidence yet, and he applied for a remand. Mr. Steel said he had no objection to a remand, but he wished to state that the prisoners were not gipsies ; they were travelling show people, and James Cook, senr., had carried on business for thirty years, and had a permanent residence in Warrington. Over £loo' damage had been done, and there was only an old lady left in charge of the horses, Scc., at the show ground. The Bench remanded the prisoners for eight days, and allowed bail, but liberated the female prisoner, who was tinning a baby, on her own recognisances.

AN EXCITING CHASE. Frederick 'inflows, a young joiner, residing at 49, Cumberland-street, Latchford, was charged at the Borough Police Court, on Saturday, with breaking and entering the lock-up shop of Messrs. G. and W. Collins, wholesale grocers, Sankey-street, on Saturday morning. It appears that early on the above morning P.C. Green, who was on duty in Sankey-street, was looking through the window of the shop and saw a man crouch- ling down near the safe. The officer had the keys to the gate leading to the side entrance, and found that a hole had been cut in one of the doors of the shop, through which the man had evidently made his way. The bele was to small that the officer could only get his shoulder through. Green afterwards forced the door oft its hinges' He then heard a noise as if slates were being removed from the roof. He rushed to the top storey end found that a large hole had been made through the roof,and the prisoner had escaped on to the roof. Word was sent to the police station, and a large body of constables assisted by employees of the Post Office and the neighbouring newspaper office surrounded the block of buildings. Nothing was seen of the man until about seven o'clock, when P.C.'s Fish and Burrows were patrolling Winraarleigh-street and saw him drop from the roof of the newspaper (Aloe. They at once gave pursuit and after a hot chase of about 300 yards they caught him and conveyed him to the Police Station. Afterwards when the premises were being examined a number of tools were found near the safe in Messrs. Collins' shop. The Chief Constable in asking for a remand until to-day, said probably six or seven other charges would be preferred against the accused. The remand w‘s granted. MUST YOU REDUCE YOUR FAT? If ion have got to the point where the excess of fat must positively be diminished—don't worry. No need to peer through the gymnasium door with a despairing glance or sniff dubiously at the uninviting basin of gruel. You can keep on eating what you please if you will but ask ycur chemist for one half- ounce of Marmola, one ounce of fluid extract of Glyeyrrbiza 8.P., one ounce of pure Glycerine 8.P., and Pepperinint Water to make six ounces in all ; then nag them it gether at home, or let your chemist mix them for you, and take a dessertspoolful after meals and at bedtime for a week or two. Get the Msrtriola in a sealed package. Good health, freedom from corpulency, and the amount of firm, smooth, flesh natural to your build will reward you. It is a very simple hut very pleasing method of regain- ing a good figure.

FRIENDLY MATCHES. WARRINGTON "A " v. CADISTINAD.—These teams met at Warrington on Saturday before a good attend- ance, several promising juniors being given a tiial in the local ranks, while the Cadishead team had in its composition a fair sprinkling of Warrington youths. Burgess' the old Warrington half-back, also figured in the ranks of the visitors. Warrington were soon off the attack, and several brilliant passing movements were initiated by Lees. Cartwright scored a good try, which Mclntyre failed to convert. For infringements at the scrimmage Cadishead were twice penalized, but Gibbs failed with the shots. A complete charge now came over the game, the visitors taking up a strong position in the home quarter. Offside pliy by the Wirepullers " enabled Bramhall to open Cadishead's account by kicking a goal. The visitors maintained the aggressive and Morley scored an uncon- verted try. Half-time arrived with Cadishead leading by five points to three. Soon after the restart Jordan secured the ball near half-way, and, beating the whole of the visiting team, scored a magnificent try, Cart- wright adding a goal. The home team were now in front and set about increasing their lead, Bradshaw scoring from a rush and Gibbs converting. Burgess made a good run, and when a try seemed certain he threw forward. A fine kick and speedy follow up t y Mclntyre was the means of further disaster to Cadis- head, the home custodian regaining the ball and scoring under the posts. Cartwright kicking the goal. Iu the closing stages Warrington again pressed home an attack and Landor scored, no goal resulting. Warrington ran out winners by three goals five tries (21 points) to One goal One try (5 points.) The Lancashire team to meet the Australi;us at Wigan, on November 25th, was selected in Manchester on 'Tuesday evening as follows :—II. Gifford (Barrow), full back; G. Tyson (Oldham), right wing, G. W. Smith (Oldham), right centre, B. Jenkins (Wigan), left centre, and J. Leytham (Wigan), left wing, three-quarter backs; J. Jolley (Runcorn) and E. Brooks (Warrington), half- backs; R.Padbury (Runcorn), W. Lon.''worth (Oldham). G. Ruddick (Broughton Rangers), A. Logan (Barrow), A Smith (Oldham), and D. flees (Salford). This is the same team that defeated Yorkshire and won the County Championship.

Dear Sir.—During the past six months we have had some extremely damp and foggy weather, and whilst hoisting in and out ocean tugs, copper punts and other small crafts, I contracted several chills one after the other, which finally settled in my legs and and feet, causing them to swell ; and to make matters worse I had the misfortune to fall out of a submerged torpedo tube,and sprained both ankles. I was placed on the sick list and given the usual naval treatment, which did me no good. Seeing your advertisement, I determined to try your famous Gout Pills, and after taking a few doses they worked marvels. All the swelling disappeared and my legs are as strong as ever again. Yours Respectfully, G. SWACKENHAMME, Q.G. EADE'S GOUT PILLS Are perfectly safe in their action, eliminate all injurious matter, and are highly restorative.
